Table 3
Developmental Plasticity: Neuronal Survival, Synapse Formation, Pruning, and Critical Periods
1. Q: What is developmental plasticity, and what does it explain about the formation of the nervous system?
A: Show answer
Developmental plasticity is the capacity of the developing nervous system to change its structure and functional connections as genetic programs interact with signals from the body and experience. The chapter explains that the brain does not emerge as a finished network whose every neuron and synapse is specified in advance. It initially produces more neurons and connections than it ultimately retains. Cells compete for survival signals, axons explore possible targets, synapses form in excess, and activity and sensory experience help stabilize useful circuits while less appropriate elements are removed. Development therefore combines construction with selective elimination. The result is a nervous system whose organization is shaped by both biological instructions and the conditions encountered during development.
Study instruction:
- "Define developmental plasticity and trace how cell survival, synapse formation, pruning, and sensory experience contribute to circuit development."
Key point:
- Development involves both growth and selective elimination.
- Genes establish important constraints, while environmental input helps refine developing circuits.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
2. Q: Why does the developing brain initially produce more neurons and synaptic connections than it needs?
A: Show answer
Overproduction creates a flexible supply of cells and connections from which functional circuits can be selected. The chapter describes a high-redundancy strategy: many neurons are generated, and immature neurons form an abundance of synaptic contacts before the nervous system removes cells and connections that are not adequately supported or stabilized. This arrangement allows developing circuits to be matched to the size and needs of their target tissues and to the patterns of activity they receive. Redundancy is therefore not simply waste. It creates developmental options, while competition, trophic support, electrical activity, and experience help determine which elements mature and persist.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why developmental overproduction can be adaptive rather than evidence of a defective or inefficient nervous system."
Key point:
- An initial surplus creates options for later refinement.
- Survival and connection patterns are selectively stabilized during maturation.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
3. Q: How do genetic instructions and environmental experience interact during neural development?
A: Show answer
Genetic programs provide the molecular machinery, developmental sequence, and constraints needed to build the nervous system. They regulate cell production, growth-factor receptors, neurotransmitter systems, and the maturation of neural circuits. However, the chapter emphasizes that early postnatal experience and the local environment help fine-tune those circuits. Target tissues provide signals that support some neurons; spontaneous activity and experience strengthen some synapses; and sensory input can influence the organization of cortical pathways. Genes and experience are therefore not competing explanations. Genetic processes establish a developmental framework, while biological signals and environmental input influence how that framework is completed.
Study instruction:
- "Identify one developmental process governed by genetic mechanisms and one way that target-derived signals or sensory experience modifies its outcome."
Key point:
- Genes and experience act together rather than independently.
- Developmental outcomes depend on both biological capacity and relevant input.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
- Torsten Wiesel: Nobel Lecture on the Visual System
4. Q: What is the neurotrophic hypothesis described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The neurotrophic hypothesis proposes that developing neurons depend on limited supplies of survival-promoting signals released by their target tissues. More neurons may initially be produced than the target environment can support. Neurons compete for these signals; cells that receive sufficient support survive and mature, whereas those that receive too little activate cell-death pathways and are eliminated. Nerve growth factor (NGF) provided a key example of such a signal. The hypothesis helped explain how the number of surviving neurons can become appropriately matched to the size and requirements of the organs and tissues they innervate. It is a developmental mechanism, not a claim that every neuron is controlled by NGF alone.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the sequence from target-derived growth-factor availability to neuronal survival or elimination."
Key point:
- Target-derived trophic signals help regulate which neurons survive.
- Limited support links the size of neuronal populations to their targets.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
5. Q: What did Viktor Hamburger observe when developing limb tissue was removed from chick embryos?
A: Show answer
Hamburger removed developing limb buds and observed that primary sensory neurons associated with the missing target tissue failed to survive in their normal numbers. These neurons normally extend fibers toward muscles and other structures in the limb. The finding showed that neuronal development depends on interactions with the tissues that neurons are intended to innervate. The chapter explains that Hamburger initially interpreted the result in terms of the importance of the target tissue for neuronal maturation. Levi-Montalcini proposed that removal of the target might instead deprive neurons of a survival signal. Their later experiments helped establish the role of target-derived trophic support.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the limb-removal experiment was important and distinguish the observation from the later explanation of its mechanism."
Key point:
- Neuronal survival is influenced by the availability of target tissue.
- The experiment led researchers to investigate signals released by target organs.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
6. Q: What did the additional-limb graft experiment reveal about neuronal survival?
A: Show answer
When an extra limb was grafted onto a chick embryo, more sensory neurons survived than in embryos with the usual target arrangement. This result complemented the limb-removal experiment. Removing a target reduced neuronal survival, while providing additional target tissue increased the number of surviving neurons. Together, the findings supported the idea that target tissues provide something that developing neurons require. The experiments did not merely show that neurons need a physical destination; they suggested that target tissue supplies a limited biological resource that affects survival. This evidence helped shift the explanation toward chemical trophic signals.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the outcomes of limb removal and supernumerary-limb grafting and explain what each experiment contributed to the hypothesis."
Key point:
- Removing targets reduced survival, while adding targets increased it.
- Complementary experiments strengthened the case for target-derived support.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
7. Q: What is nerve growth factor (NGF), and what does it do during neural development?
A: Show answer
Nerve growth factor is a diffusible protein that supports the survival, growth, and differentiation of particular populations of neurons. In the chapter's account, NGF is secreted by certain tissues and acts on responsive nerve cells. Its availability helps determine whether developing neurons survive and mature, and it can stimulate nerve-fiber growth. NGF was the first identified neurotrophic growth factor and became a foundational example of chemical communication between target tissues and developing neurons. It should not be confused with a universal nutrient for every neuron: different neuronal populations depend on different trophic factors and receptor systems.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why NGF is described as a neurotrophic factor and identify the developmental functions supported by the chapter."
Key point:
- NGF is a protein signal that supports selected neuronal populations.
- Neurotrophic factors help coordinate survival and differentiation with tissue needs.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- Nobel Prize: Nerve Signalling – Tracing the Wiring of Life
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
8. Q: How did Rita Levi-Montalcini and Stanley Cohen identify NGF?
A: Show answer
Levi-Montalcini investigated why nerve fibers grew toward transplanted tumors in chick embryos and proposed that the tumors released a diffusible survival or growth signal. Working with Stanley Cohen, she tested substances that might reveal the nature of that signal. Snake venom unexpectedly stimulated substantial nerve-fiber outgrowth in cultured sensory neurons. This observation led them to investigate mouse salivary glands, which were found to contain abundant activity of the same kind. They purified the active substance and established that it was a small protein, which they named nerve growth factor. Their work transformed an observation about nerve growth into the identification of a specific chemical signal.
Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the discovery sequence from tumor-associated nerve growth through the snake-venom observation and purification from salivary glands."
Key point:
- The discovery depended on following an unexpected experimental result.
- NGF was identified through a sequence of observation, hypothesis, testing, and purification.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- Nobel Prize: Nerve Signalling – Tracing the Wiring of Life
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
9. Q: Why was the snake-venom experiment an important turning point in the discovery of NGF?
A: Show answer
Snake venom was not initially expected to be a source of the activity being investigated. When it was added to sensory neurons growing in culture, it produced more nerve-fiber outgrowth than the tumor material that had prompted the research. The unexpected result suggested that the venom contained a substance capable of stimulating neuronal growth. Cohen then proposed investigating mouse salivary glands, which are biologically related to venom glands and proved to be a rich source of the activity. The episode illustrates how an anomalous result can redirect a research program when investigators examine it rather than dismissing it as experimental noise.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how an unexpected result can generate a new experimental hypothesis, using the snake-venom observation as the example."
Key point:
- Unexpected observations can reveal an unrecognized source of biological activity.
- Scientific progress often depends on testing, not ignoring, surprising results.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
10. Q: How did anti-NGF antibodies help establish the biological role of nerve growth factor?
A: Show answer
After purifying NGF, Cohen and Levi-Montalcini produced antibodies against it. They then showed that these antibodies blocked the protein's effects in newborn rodents and in nerve cells growing in culture. This was important because it went beyond observing that a preparation stimulated growth. If neutralizing the identified protein removed the biological effect, the evidence supported the conclusion that NGF itself was responsible. The experiments strengthened the causal link between the molecule and neuronal survival or growth. More generally, neutralization experiments can help test whether a candidate signaling molecule is necessary for an observed biological response.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why blocking a candidate molecule with a specific antibody provides stronger causal evidence than observing an association alone."
Key point:
- Neutralization tests whether a candidate signal is responsible for an effect.
- Blocking NGF helped establish its role in neuronal development.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
11. Q: How do neurotrophic factors communicate with developing neurons?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes neurotrophic factors binding to receptor proteins on responsive cells. The growth-factor–receptor complex is then internalized and transported toward the cell body and nucleus, where it can influence genetic programs by switching particular genes on or off. This provides a mechanism through which signals from target tissues can affect neuronal survival, differentiation, and maturation. The chapter also notes that many neurotrophic factors and their receptors have been identified since NGF. The important principle is that extracellular signals can be converted into intracellular changes that alter the developmental state of a neuron.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the pathway from growth-factor release to receptor binding, internalization, and changes in gene activity."
Key point:
- External trophic signals can change gene activity inside a neuron.
- Receptor-mediated signaling links the target environment to cell development.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
12. Q: Why is NGF considered a milestone in the history of developmental neuroscience?
A: Show answer
NGF was the first growth factor identified and provided direct evidence that tissues can release chemical signals that regulate the survival and development of nerve cells. Its discovery helped establish the broader neurotrophic concept and encouraged researchers to search for other factors that support different neuronal populations. The work also connected experimental embryology, cell culture, biochemistry, and molecular biology. Levi-Montalcini and Cohen shared the 1986 Nobel Prize in Physiology or Medicine for discoveries concerning growth factors. The significance lies not only in one molecule but in the general insight that neural development is regulated by communication between cells and their environment.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the scientific contribution of NGF beyond its specific effect on sensory neurons."
Key point:
- NGF established a wider principle of chemical regulation in neural development.
- Its discovery opened a field of research into multiple neurotrophic factors.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- Nobel Prize: Nerve Signalling – Tracing the Wiring of Life
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
13. Q: What is programmed cell death, and why does it occur during normal brain development?
A: Show answer
Programmed cell death is a genetically regulated process through which cells are deliberately eliminated as part of normal development. The chapter explains that developing nervous systems produce excess neurons and then remove cells that do not receive adequate survival support. This process helps regulate the size of neuronal populations, the spacing and positioning of cells, and the formation of appropriate neural structures. It is therefore not automatically a sign of injury or disease. When controlled within the developmental program, cell death is part of building a functional nervous system and helps match neuronal populations to their targets and developmental requirements.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish normal developmental cell death from cell loss caused by injury or disease, and explain its organizational purpose."
Key point:
- Selective cell elimination is a normal developmental mechanism.
- Controlled cell death helps shape neuronal populations and tissue organization.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
14. Q: How does limited neurotrophic support lead to programmed neuronal death?
A: Show answer
In the chapter's account, developing neurons compete for a limited supply of target-derived neurotrophic factors. Cells that receive sufficient signaling activate survival and maturation pathways. When a neuron does not receive adequate support, the absence of trophic signaling can allow cell-death genes to become active. The cell then enters a genetically regulated dismantling process. The mechanism helps ensure that neuronal survival is not independent of the size and needs of the tissues being innervated. The chapter presents NGF as an important example, while noting that other neurotrophic factors support other neuronal populations.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how competition for target-derived survival signals can regulate the number of neurons that persist."
Key point:
- Survival depends partly on receiving adequate trophic support.
- Neurons lacking sufficient support may activate programmed elimination pathways.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Rita Levi-Montalcini and the Discovery of Nerve Growth Factor
15. Q: What role do caspases play in programmed cell death during neural development?
A: Show answer
Caspases are enzymes encoded by genes that participate in the execution of programmed cell death. The chapter describes them as cellular executioners: once the death program is activated, caspases help dismantle internal cellular components. They contribute to the breakdown of DNA and structural proteins, producing structural changes associated with a dying cell. Their activity is part of a regulated sequence rather than random deterioration. In developmental context, caspase activation helps remove cells that are no longer supported or required. The chapter focuses on their role in cell elimination; it does not imply that caspases are the only regulators of every developmental death event.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the place of caspases in the sequence from loss of survival signaling to cellular dismantling."
Key point:
- Caspases execute key stages of programmed cell dismantling.
- Their activation is part of a regulated genetic process.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
16. Q: What physical changes occur when a neuron undergoes programmed cell death?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes several characteristic changes after the cell-death program is activated. Caspase activity contributes to fragmentation of DNA and structural proteins. Chromosomes condense, the cell shrinks, and the cell membrane develops blebs. These changes give the dying cell a recognizable appearance and help separate the process from uncontrolled rupture. The sequence reflects an organized dismantling of cellular structures. The chapter's emphasis is that developmental cell death is an active biological program, not simply a passive disappearance of neurons that fail to mature.
Study instruction:
- "List the morphological changes described in the chapter and connect them to the regulated breakdown of cellular components."
Key point:
- Programmed death involves an ordered series of structural changes.
- DNA fragmentation, shrinkage, and membrane blebbing are characteristic features described in the chapter.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
17. Q: How are dead neuronal cells cleared from developing nervous tissue?
A: Show answer
After a cell has been dismantled, immune cells called macrophages engulf and clear the cellular debris. This clearance step is important because cellular remnants must be removed as tissue develops and reorganizes. The chapter places macrophage activity at the end of the described programmed-cell-death sequence, after caspase-mediated dismantling has altered the cell's structure. The point is not that macrophages decide which neuron survives; rather, they help dispose of the material left by cells that have already entered the death process. Clearance is therefore part of orderly tissue maintenance during development.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the difference between the signaling that initiates cell death, the caspase-mediated dismantling, and macrophage clearance."
Key point:
- Macrophages remove debris after cellular dismantling.
- Clearance supports orderly remodeling of developing tissue.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
18. Q: What is synaptogenesis, and why is it important in developmental plasticity?
A: Show answer
Synaptogenesis is the formation of synaptic connections between nerve cells or between a nerve cell and another target, such as a muscle fiber. The chapter describes immature neurons as initially forming many more contacts than are ultimately retained. These connections provide the structural basis for communication within neural circuits. As development proceeds, some contacts are stabilized by activity, trophic support, and experience, while others are weakened or eliminated. Synaptogenesis is therefore only one part of circuit construction; the initial creation of contacts must be followed by maturation and selective refinement before a stable functional network emerges.
Study instruction:
- "Define synaptogenesis and explain why connection formation must be followed by maturation and refinement."
Key point:
- Synaptogenesis creates the initial communication network.
- Functional circuits emerge through both connection formation and later selection.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
19. Q: Why are immature neurons described as forming synapses promiscuously?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses this description to convey that immature neurons initially form an abundance of synaptic contacts, including connections that will not remain in the mature circuit. This broad early connectivity gives developing neurons multiple possible partners and routes for communication. Subsequent processes select among these contacts. Spontaneous electrical activity, experience, and the availability of growth factors help stabilize some connections, while less active or inadequately supported branches are retracted. The term does not imply random or meaningless development; it describes a stage in which the network is more exuberant than the final arrangement.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what is meant by exuberant early connectivity and how later refinement changes it."
Key point:
- Early connectivity is broader than the mature network.
- Activity and support help determine which connections persist.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
20. Q: Why is the neuromuscular junction useful for studying synapse formation?
A: Show answer
The neuromuscular junction, where a motor-neuron terminal contacts skeletal muscle, is relatively accessible and easier to examine than the small, densely packed synapses within the brain. The chapter notes that Santiago Ramón y Cajal recognized the value of studying this more accessible developing system to understand synaptic formation. At the neuromuscular junction, researchers can observe how a growing nerve fiber reaches muscle, how acetylcholine receptors become concentrated at the contact site, and how multiple early connections are refined. The junction therefore provides a tractable model for mechanisms that are believed to operate in broadly similar ways in central nervous system synapses.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a relatively accessible peripheral synapse can help researchers investigate general principles of neural development."
Key point:
- The neuromuscular junction offers an accessible model of synaptic development.
- Its visible maturation helps reveal mechanisms that are harder to study in the brain.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
21. Q: What is a growth cone, and how do filopodia help a developing nerve fiber find its target?
A: Show answer
A growth cone is the dynamic structure at the tip of a developing nerve fiber. The chapter describes it as having finger-like projections called filopodia. These projections help the growing tip detect chemical cues in its local environment and guide the direction of extension. As the growth cone advances, the nerve fiber lays down new material and extends toward an appropriate destination. This exploratory process allows developing axons to navigate through tissue rather than growing as fixed cables. Guidance cues and interactions with target tissues help determine where the developing connection will form.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the structure of a growth cone and explain how filopodia contribute to target-directed growth."
Key point:
- Growth cones explore the local environment as axons extend.
- Filopodia help detect cues that guide the growing nerve fiber.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
22. Q: How does acetylcholine function at the developing neuromuscular junction?
A: Show answer
At the neuromuscular junction, the motor neuron releases acetylcholine, a neurotransmitter that binds to acetylcholine receptors on muscle fibers and causes them to contract. During development, acetylcholine signaling is also involved in organizing the contact between nerve and muscle. As the growing nerve terminal approaches the muscle, it releases acetylcholine, which contributes to the redistribution and clustering of receptors beneath the muscle membrane. The mature junction therefore depends on coordinated development of the nerve terminal, the muscle membrane, and the receptor arrangement that enables effective signaling.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the normal signaling sequence from motor-neuron release of acetylcholine to muscle contraction and receptor organization during development."
Key point:
- Acetylcholine is both a transmitter for muscle activation and part of junctional maturation.
- Nerve and muscle development are coordinated through signaling interactions.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
23. Q: How do acetylcholine receptors become concentrated at the motor endplate?
A: Show answer
In the immature muscle, acetylcholine receptors are initially distributed broadly beneath the membrane. As the growing nerve terminal approaches and releases acetylcholine, the receptors begin to redistribute and cluster at particular locations. The chapter explains that these clusters subsequently become immobilized, creating a specialized receptor zone. As the muscle mass separates into individual fibers, each fiber develops an endplate with a high density of receptors. This concentration makes the mature neuromuscular junction an efficient site for communication between the motor neuron and muscle.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a broadly distributed receptor population becomes concentrated at a specialized synaptic contact zone."
Key point:
- Receptor clustering creates a specialized postsynaptic region.
- The endplate concentrates receptors where nerve-to-muscle signaling occurs.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
24. Q: How does nerve innervation change the muscle's acetylcholine receptor population?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes several effects of innervation. Contact with the motor neuron increases the conductance of receptors already present in the muscle and stimulates the synthesis of additional receptor molecules, which are inserted into the muscle membrane. Together with receptor clustering and immobilization, these changes help create a specialized endplate. The developing muscle is therefore not simply waiting for a mature nerve terminal to arrive; the interaction between nerve and muscle actively changes the molecular organization of both partners. Synaptic maturation is a reciprocal process in which each side influences the development of the other.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the effects of innervation on existing receptors, new receptor synthesis, and receptor placement."
Key point:
- Innervation actively organizes the postsynaptic muscle membrane.
- Synaptic maturation involves reciprocal signaling between nerve and muscle.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
25. Q: What is an endplate, and why is its receptor density important?
A: Show answer
An endplate is the specialized region of the muscle membrane at the neuromuscular junction where acetylcholine receptors are concentrated. The chapter reports that a mature endplate contains approximately 20,000 acetylcholine receptors per square micrometer, several thousand times the density found in other regions of the muscle membrane. This concentration places a large number of receptors directly opposite the nerve terminal, supporting effective transmission of the motor signal. The numerical value illustrates the degree of specialization achieved during development; it should be understood as the chapter's reported estimate rather than a universal measurement for every species, muscle, or experimental condition.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why receptor concentration at the endplate improves the organization of neuromuscular communication."
Key point:
- The endplate is a highly specialized receptor-rich membrane region.
- Receptor density is much greater at the synaptic contact than elsewhere on the muscle membrane.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
26. Q: How do spontaneous electrical activity and experience influence the survival of developing synapses?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that early motor-neuron branches may contact more than one muscle fiber. As development proceeds, spontaneous electrical activity helps stabilize some of these connections, and experience strengthens them further. Connections that are not sufficiently reinforced are more likely to be withdrawn. This means that the developing circuit is refined through use-related signals rather than simply retaining every contact that forms. The chapter also links this process to the availability of growth factors in muscle cells. Activity, experience, and trophic support therefore interact in determining which synaptic connections mature.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how spontaneous activity, later experience, and trophic support contribute to selective stabilization of synapses."
Key point:
- Activity helps select and strengthen developing connections.
- Less-supported branches can be withdrawn as circuits mature.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
27. Q: Why are some early motor-neuron branches retracted during development?
A: Show answer
Early in development, a motor neuron may send branches to more than one muscle fiber. As the system matures, the number of connections decreases. Branches that do not receive sufficient growth-factor support may be retracted, while connections strengthened by electrical activity and experience are more likely to persist. The chapter describes the mature outcome as one motor neuron innervating a single muscle fiber at the junctions discussed. The broader principle is that early overconnection is refined through competition and selective stabilization. Retraction is therefore a normal part of organizing an effective mature circuit.
Study instruction:
- "Trace how multiple early motor-neuron contacts are reduced to a more selective mature arrangement."
Key point:
- Branch retraction removes connections that are not adequately supported.
- Mature organization is produced through selective refinement, not simply by adding more contacts.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
28. Q: How do synapses in the brain and spinal cord differ from the neuromuscular junction?
A: Show answer
At the neuromuscular junction, a nerve terminal communicates with a muscle fiber. In the brain and spinal cord, synapses connect neuronal elements to one another: a nerve terminal may contact a cell body, an axon, or a dendrite. The chapter also contrasts the mature motor-neuron arrangement at the muscle junction with the extensive connectivity of central neurons, which are estimated to form an average of about 10,000 synaptic connections with other cells. Central synapses are smaller, more densely packed, and harder to study directly, although their formation is believed to follow broadly similar principles.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the target cells, structural arrangements, and relative accessibility of central synapses and neuromuscular junctions."
Key point:
- Central synapses connect neurons to other neurons, while the neuromuscular junction connects nerve to muscle.
- Central circuits have extensive and densely packed connectivity.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
29. Q: When does synapse formation begin in humans, and how does it vary across brain regions?
A: Show answer
The chapter states that synapse formation begins during embryonic development and continues during the early postnatal period. Functional synapses have been observed in humans at approximately 23 weeks of gestation. The timing is not uniform across the brain: different regions form and stabilize connections at different rates. The chapter reports that synaptic numbers in many regions peak during the first year of life, while some areas of the frontal cortex continue producing synapses well into the third year. These figures describe developmental patterns reported in the literature discussed by the author; they should not be treated as exact milestones for every individual.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the timing of early human synaptogenesis and explain why a single age cannot describe every brain region."
Key point:
- Synaptogenesis begins before birth and continues after birth.
- Brain regions follow different developmental schedules.

- Review: Adolescence as a Neurobiological Critical Period
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
30. Q: What does the chapter say about the timing of synaptic density in the visual cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the visual cortex as an example of region-specific development. It reports that the formation and stabilization of synapses in this region depend strongly on visual experience and that synaptic connections reach their highest density between approximately 2.5 and 8 months of age. This period of early overproduction provides a foundation for subsequent activity-dependent refinement. The timing is an estimate drawn from the studies discussed in the chapter, not a diagnostic timetable for an individual infant. The important point is that visual experience arrives while the developing cortical circuit is especially receptive to input.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the visual cortex is a useful example of experience-dependent synaptic development and identify the age range reported in the chapter."
Key point:
- Visual experience is important while visual-cortex connections are being refined.
- The chapter reports peak visual-cortex synaptic density at roughly 2.5–8 months.

31. Q: Why do some frontal cortical regions continue forming synapses later than sensory regions?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that some regions of the developing frontal cortex continue creating synapses well into the third year of life. This contrasts with the earlier peaks reported for many other regions. The difference reflects the fact that brain regions have distinct developmental trajectories and that circuits supporting complex functions can undergo prolonged construction and refinement. Later development should not be interpreted as a defect or as evidence that all frontal functions are absent in early childhood. It indicates that the organization of these circuits continues to change over an extended period, with experience and maturation contributing to their development.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the developmental timing of the visual cortex and frontal cortex described in the chapter."
Key point:
- Different cortical regions mature on different schedules.
- Some frontal circuits continue forming connections after many other regions have reached earlier peaks.

32. Q: What is synaptic pruning, and how does it differ from synaptogenesis?
A: Show answer
Synaptic pruning is the selective elimination of synaptic connections that are not retained as neural circuits mature. Synaptogenesis creates new contacts; pruning removes a subset of those contacts after or during their formation. The chapter describes immature neurons as initially making more connections than they need, followed by a reduction in the number of synapses as the system becomes more specialized. Pruning is not simply random destruction. It is influenced by activity, experience, and the developmental mechanisms that stabilize selected connections. Both formation and elimination are necessary for constructing an appropriately organized network.
Study instruction:
- "Define synaptogenesis and synaptic pruning, then explain why both processes are required for neural development."
Key point:
- Synaptogenesis builds connections; pruning selectively removes some of them.
- Mature circuitry depends on both addition and elimination.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
33. Q: Why is synaptic pruning necessary for the development of functional neural circuits?
A: Show answer
An immature network contains many redundant, weak, or mismatched connections. If every early contact remained, the resulting circuitry would not have the same refined organization as the mature system. Pruning reduces excess connections and helps organize the pattern of communication among neurons. The chapter explains that this refinement is influenced by electrical activity and experience, allowing some connections to be stabilized while others are removed. Pruning should not be interpreted as a general loss of intelligence or as damage; it is a normal developmental process that contributes to specialization and efficiency of neural circuits.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how eliminating selected connections can improve the organization of a developing neural network."
Key point:
- Circuit refinement requires selective elimination as well as growth.
- Pruning helps remove redundant or less-appropriate connections.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
- Review: Adolescence as a Neurobiological Critical Period
34. Q: How long does synaptic pruning continue in the human prefrontal cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts older views, which placed most cortical pruning around puberty and early adolescence, with evidence that pruning in the prefrontal cortex continues well into the third decade of life. The prefrontal cortex therefore undergoes a prolonged period of refinement. The exact trajectory varies across people and brain regions, and the chapter's age description should not be treated as a sharp birthday at which maturation suddenly ends. The key point is that structural development continues through adolescence and into early adulthood, rather than being completed at the end of childhood.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how the chapter updates the earlier view that cortical pruning ends in early adolescence."
Key point:
- Prefrontal refinement extends beyond adolescence into early adulthood.
- Brain maturation is gradual and region-specific, not a single event.

- Review: Synaptic Plasticity and the Development of Executive Function
35. Q: How is prefrontal synaptic pruning related to decision-making and executive function?
A: Show answer
The chapter links the prefrontal cortex with complex functions such as decision-making and evaluating rewards. As its circuitry is refined, executive functions improve. These functions support planning, evaluating consequences, regulating behavior, and making decisions in relation to longer-term goals. The chapter uses this relationship to help explain why adolescents may place strong emphasis on peer approval and may engage in risky behavior to obtain it. This is a developmental association, not a claim that every adolescent behaves in the same way or that pruning alone explains behavior. Experience, social context, and many other biological factors also matter.
Study instruction:
- "Connect the prolonged development of prefrontal circuitry with the gradual maturation of executive functions."
Key point:
- Prefrontal development is associated with improving executive control.
- Adolescent behavior should be interpreted in developmental and social context.

- Review: Adolescence as a Neurobiological Critical Period
- Review: Synaptic Plasticity and the Development of Executive Function
36. Q: Does synaptic pruning stop when a person reaches adulthood?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter explicitly states that the adult brain continues to form new synapses and eliminate unwanted ones throughout life. Developmental pruning is especially extensive during early life and adolescence, but synaptic formation and elimination are not confined to childhood. In adulthood, these processes contribute to learning, memory, and normal brain function. This distinction prevents two opposite misconceptions: that the brain is fully fixed after development, and that adult plasticity is identical in scale or conditions to early developmental plasticity. The mechanisms continue, but their rates, constraints, and functional contexts vary.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish the extensive refinement of developing circuits from the ongoing synaptic changes that occur in adulthood."
Key point:
- Synaptic remodeling continues after developmental periods.
- Adult plasticity persists, although its context and degree can differ from early development.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
37. Q: What did Hubel and Wiesel's experiments reveal about the effect of visual experience on cortical development?
A: Show answer
Hubel and Wiesel studied neurons in the primary visual cortex and showed that early visual experience influences how cortical circuits respond to input from the two eyes. Their experiments included raising kittens with one eyelid sutured shut. The visual cortex of animals deprived of input from one eye developed differently from that of normally reared animals: cortical representation shifted toward the eye that remained open. The work demonstrated that sensory experience is not merely used by an already completed visual system; it helps shape the developing organization of that system. The experiments became central evidence for experience-dependent plasticity and critical periods.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the monocular-deprivation experiment and explain what it demonstrated about developing visual pathways."
Key point:
- Visual experience helps organize the developing visual cortex.
- Early sensory input can alter which connections are strengthened.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Torsten Wiesel: Nobel Lecture on the Visual System
- David Hubel: Nobel Lecture on the Primary Visual Cortex
38. Q: What are ocular-dominance columns in the primary visual cortex?
A: Show answer
Ocular-dominance columns are alternating regions of the primary visual cortex that respond preferentially to input from one eye or the other. The chapter explains that inputs from the two eyes converge in the visual cortex and compete for representation. These columns form part of the organized pattern through which visual information is represented. Their development is influenced by visual experience: when one eye is deprived during the relevant developmental period, the columns that would normally receive input from that eye fail to develop normally, while those associated with the open eye expand. The concept illustrates how sensory input can shape cortical organization.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how inputs from the two eyes are represented in ocular-dominance columns and how deprivation changes their development."
Key point:
- Ocular-dominance columns reflect the organization of input from the two eyes.
- Their development is sensitive to early visual experience.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Torsten Wiesel: Nobel Lecture on the Visual System
- David Hubel: Nobel Lecture on the Primary Visual Cortex
39. Q: What happened when Hubel and Wiesel sutured one eyelid of a newborn kitten?
A: Show answer
The kittens were deprived of patterned visual input through one eye while the other eye remained open. The chapter reports that cortical columns that would normally receive input from the closed eye failed to develop as expected, while those receiving input from the open eye became larger than normal. This showed that the two sets of inputs compete for cortical representation and that the outcome depends on sensory experience during a particular developmental window. The experiment was performed in animals and should be interpreted as evidence about developmental mechanisms, not as a direct clinical protocol for human children.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the cortical changes caused by monocular deprivation and identify the role of the open eye."
Key point:
- Deprivation shifts cortical representation toward the eye receiving input.
- The timing of deprivation determines how strongly the developing system is affected.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Torsten Wiesel: Nobel Lecture on the Visual System
- David Hubel: Nobel Lecture on the Primary Visual Cortex
40. Q: What is a critical period in sensory development?
A: Show answer
A critical period is a relatively narrow developmental window during which the nervous system is especially sensitive to particular environmental or sensory input. The chapter introduces the concept through visual-cortex experiments: deprivation of one eye produced marked changes when it occurred early, while the same intervention later had much less effect. During the window, experience can strongly influence the formation and stabilization of neural connections. The term does not mean that learning or plasticity becomes impossible after the period closes. It describes a phase of heightened sensitivity for a particular system and type of input.
Study instruction:
- "Define a critical period and distinguish heightened developmental sensitivity from the idea that plasticity ends permanently afterward."
Key point:
- Critical periods are windows of heightened sensitivity to particular input.
- Their timing and effects differ across neural systems.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Torsten Wiesel: Nobel Lecture on the Visual System
- David Hubel: Nobel Lecture on the Primary Visual Cortex
- Review: Adolescence as a Neurobiological Critical Period
41. Q: Why does the timing of sensory deprivation matter during a critical period?
A: Show answer
Developing neural circuits are especially receptive to input during critical periods. If a sensory pathway receives unequal or inadequate input during that window, competing connections may be strengthened or weakened in ways that alter the mature representation. The chapter's kitten experiments showed that the effect of closing one eye depended on the animal's age and that reopening the eye could reverse the changes only if it occurred before a certain developmental stage. Timing therefore matters because the same experience can have different effects depending on the maturity and stability of the circuit when it occurs.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the same sensory deprivation may have a different effect in an immature and a mature visual system."
Key point:
- The effect of experience depends on the developmental state of the circuit.
- Early deprivation can alter circuit organization more strongly than later deprivation.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Torsten Wiesel: Nobel Lecture on the Visual System
- David Hubel: Nobel Lecture on the Primary Visual Cortex
42. Q: What does the reversibility of monocular-deprivation effects tell us about critical periods?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that the effects of closing one eye could be reversed if the eye was reopened before the kittens reached a certain age. This shows that the developing visual cortex remains capable of reorganizing during part of the critical period, but that the capacity becomes more restricted as the circuit matures. Reversibility is therefore time-dependent rather than unlimited. The result also emphasizes that critical periods are not merely moments when damage occurs; they are windows during which the developing system can be shaped by experience and, under some conditions, can recover from altered input.
Study instruction:
- "Explain what early reversibility and later reduced reversibility reveal about the changing plasticity of a developing circuit."
Key point:
- Plasticity is especially strong during a limited developmental window.
- Reopening the eye was effective only when it occurred early enough in the experiments.

43. Q: How did Hubel and Wiesel's work contribute to the understanding and treatment of amblyopia?
A: Show answer
Amblyopia, commonly called lazy eye, is a developmental condition in which vision in one eye is reduced because the visual pathway has not developed normally. The chapter explains that the monocular-deprivation experiments helped establish the importance of early visual input and informed treatment approaches. Patching the stronger eye forces the child to use the weaker eye, providing the deprived pathway with visual stimulation. The chapter reports that the best outcomes are generally achieved when treatment begins before about eight years of age. This is a broad developmental statement from the chapter, not a substitute for individual diagnosis or current clinical advice.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the rationale for patching the stronger eye and why timing matters in amblyopia treatment."
Key point:
- Patching aims to increase use of the weaker visual pathway.
- Treatment effectiveness is influenced by the child's developmental stage and clinical circumstances.

44. Q: Why should the age range for amblyopia treatment not be treated as a rigid universal cutoff?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that the best outcome is achieved when treatment begins before about eight years of age, reflecting the importance of developmental timing. However, a single age should not be interpreted as a universal biological switch that applies identically to every child or every clinical situation. The timing and choice of treatment depend on the cause and severity of amblyopia, the child's visual development, adherence, and professional assessment. The experimental findings explain why early intervention matters; they do not replace individualized ophthalmic evaluation or imply that later treatment is always futile.

45. Q: Do critical periods apply only to the visual system?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter states that other sensory systems are also dependent on experience for their development. The visual system is a particularly well-studied example because researchers could manipulate visual input and measure changes in cortical responses. The broader principle is that sensory pathways develop through interactions between biological maturation and the information they receive. The timing and mechanisms differ among systems, so findings from visual cortex should not automatically be applied to hearing, touch, language, or other functions without relevant evidence. The chapter uses visual research to introduce a general developmental principle, not to claim that every system has identical critical-period timing.

46. Q: What role do inhibitory interneurons and GABA play in the maturation of critical-period plasticity?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that the timing of visual-cortex plasticity is controlled in part by the maturation of inhibitory interneurons. These neurons release gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), which inhibits neuronal activity and helps regulate the activity of local networks. Their maturation changes the balance of excitation and inhibition in the developing cortex. As inhibitory circuits mature, they contribute to the refinement and stabilization of synaptic connections and help bring the critical period toward closure. The chapter emphasizes that critical-period timing is therefore not determined only by the arrival of sensory experience; it also depends on the maturation of neural mechanisms that regulate circuit activity.

- GABA is central to the maturation of the visual system described in the chapter.

47. Q: What are large basket cells, and why are they important in the chapter's account of visual-cortex development?
A: Show answer
Large basket cells are a type of inhibitory interneuron in the primary visual cortex. The chapter identifies them as important for the development of plasticity in the visual system because they mature gradually and help refine inhibitory connections. Their maturation is associated with the timing of the critical period. As these cells develop, they stabilize some connections and contribute to the organization of the local cortical network. The chapter uses them to illustrate how a specific interneuron population can influence the period during which sensory experience has a strong effect on circuit development.

48. Q: What is Otx2, and how does it contribute to the maturation of large basket cells?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes Otx2 as a protein transported from the retina along the optic nerve to the visual cortex after newborn mice open their eyes. It accumulates inside large basket cells. When its concentration reaches a sufficient level, Otx2 enters the nucleus and activates a genetic program that promotes maturation of these interneurons. This sequence links visual experience and signals from the retina to the development of inhibitory circuits in the cortex. The chapter presents Otx2 as one mechanism involved in timing the maturation of large basket cells, not as the only factor governing all critical-period plasticity.

49. Q: How do extracellular matrix structures contribute to the stabilization of developing visual-cortex circuits?
A: Show answer
As large basket cells mature, the chapter explains that their developing network becomes surrounded by a net of extracellular matrix proteins. This structure helps strengthen and stabilize the newly refined synaptic connections. The net therefore contributes to a reduction in the circuit's capacity for large-scale reorganization. In the chapter's account, sensory experience helps refine the visual cortex, interneuron maturation supports this process, and extracellular matrix structures help consolidate the resulting circuitry. Stabilization is useful because it preserves a more accurate representation of the environment, although it also reduces the degree of plasticity associated with the earlier critical period.

- Stabilization reduces the ease of large-scale developmental reorganization.

50. Q: How do brain-derived neurotrophic factor (BDNF) and GABA-related activity influence critical-period timing?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that BDNF is a growth factor needed for the survival and maturation of large basket cells. Infusing BDNF into the visual cortex accelerated closure of the critical period in the experiments described. It also notes that reducing GABA-mediated inhibition prevented normal experience-dependent plasticity of ocular-dominance columns in mice. These findings show that growth-factor signaling and inhibitory activity help regulate the timing and expression of plasticity. They are experimental observations about developmental mechanisms, not instructions for using growth factors or drugs to alter human brain development.

Key point:
- BDNF and inhibitory signaling influence the timing of visual-circuit maturation.

51. Q: What experimental findings suggest that critical periods may be reopened after they close?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes several animal experiments that altered mechanisms involved in stabilizing visual-cortex circuits. Reducing GABA-mediated inhibition, injecting an enzyme that breaks down the extracellular matrix net, or transplanting immature interneurons into the brains of newborn mice produced changes that restored or induced periods of plasticity in the experimental models. These findings challenged the idea that critical periods are absolutely irreversible. However, the results are experimental and largely based on animal work. They do not establish a safe or effective method for reopening critical periods in people, and they should not be used to justify unsupervised medication or intervention.

52. Q: Why does the chapter say that critical periods are not as critical as once thought?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses this phrase to reflect evidence that the mechanisms controlling the timing and closure of critical periods can sometimes be modified. Maturation of inhibitory circuits, growth-factor signaling, and extracellular matrix stabilization all contribute to the reduction of plasticity. Experiments that alter these mechanisms have reopened or extended plasticity in animal models. The phrase does not mean that timing is unimportant or that every developmental effect can be reversed. It means that the closure of a critical period is biologically regulated rather than necessarily an irreversible, unexplained endpoint. Translating this insight into safe human treatments requires additional evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the distinction between a biologically regulated critical-period closure and an assumption of permanent irreversibility."
Key point:
- Critical-period closure depends on mechanisms that can be experimentally altered.
- Evidence of reopening does not mean all developmental effects are reversible.

53. Q: What is the difference between a critical period and a general capacity for lifelong plasticity?
A: Show answer
A critical period is a particular developmental window in which a system is unusually sensitive to specific input. Lifelong plasticity refers more broadly to the nervous system's continuing ability to change through learning, experience, and adaptation after early development. The chapter shows that both can be true: early visual-cortex circuits have a period of heightened sensitivity, yet synapse formation and elimination continue in adult brains. The distinction concerns degree, timing, and mechanisms. A critical period is not the only time when change is possible; it is a phase when certain forms of experience-dependent organization occur especially readily.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the heightened sensitivity of a critical period with the continuing plasticity described for adult brains."
Key point:
- Critical periods describe heightened sensitivity, not the only possible period of change.
- Adult synaptic remodeling continues beyond early developmental windows.

54. Q: How should findings from animal experiments be translated into conclusions about human development?
A: Show answer
Animal experiments allow researchers to control sensory input, manipulate molecular pathways, and examine neural tissue in ways that are often impossible in human studies. The chapter uses kitten and mouse experiments to explain mechanisms of ocular-dominance development, interneuron maturation, and critical-period regulation. These studies provide important mechanistic evidence, but species differ in developmental timing, brain organization, and clinical context. A result in a kitten or mouse cannot automatically establish the same age range, treatment, or effect in a child. Human conclusions require converging evidence from clinical, developmental, imaging, and other research, interpreted with appropriate caution.
Study instruction:

Key point:
- Animal models clarify mechanisms but do not automatically determine human clinical practice.
- Species and developmental differences must be considered when interpreting evidence.

55. Q: What are the main risks of oversimplifying developmental plasticity in parenting or education advice?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that experience influences developing circuits, but this does not mean that every experience permanently determines a child's abilities or that a single intervention can precisely shape a particular neural outcome. Development involves many interacting factors, including genetic programs, target-derived signals, spontaneous activity, sensory input, and the maturation of inhibitory circuits. Critical periods are specific to particular systems and do not provide a universal timetable for every skill. Claims that a particular activity will guarantee intelligence, prevent a condition, or permanently rewire the brain go beyond the evidence presented. Developmental findings should inform thoughtful support, not simplistic promises or fear-based claims.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a claim that one activity or experience will permanently determine a child's brain development and identify what evidence would be needed."
Key point:
- Experience matters, but developmental outcomes are multi-factorial.
- Critical-period research should not be converted into exaggerated promises or rigid parenting rules.

56. Q: How can the chapter's developmental mechanisms be organized into one integrated sequence?
A: Show answer
The chapter can be understood as a sequence of interacting processes. First, developing nervous systems produce an excess of neurons and connections. Second, target tissues release neurotrophic factors that support selected neurons. Third, cells that do not receive adequate survival signals may undergo programmed cell death, with caspases dismantling the cell and macrophages clearing debris. Fourth, surviving neurons extend axons and form synapses, initially in excess. Fifth, electrical activity, growth factors, and sensory experience stabilize selected contacts and allow others to be retracted. Finally, inhibitory-circuit maturation and extracellular matrix structures help consolidate some circuits and reduce heightened critical-period plasticity. The sequence is a conceptual map; processes overlap in time and differ across regions.
Study instruction:
- "Create a flow diagram linking overproduction, trophic support, cell death, synaptogenesis, pruning, sensory experience, and circuit stabilization."
Key point:
- Neural development combines survival selection, connection formation, and activity-dependent refinement.
- The processes overlap and operate at different times across brain regions.

- Lasker Foundation: Growth Factors – NGF and EGF
- PubMed: Mechanisms Governing Activity-Dependent Synaptic Pruning
- Torsten Wiesel: Nobel Lecture on the Visual System
57. Q: What evidence would help distinguish a normal developmental change from a neurological problem?
A: Show answer
A developmental mechanism described in the chapter cannot be used by itself to diagnose an individual. Normal processes such as programmed cell death, synaptic pruning, and changing sensory-circuit organization occur within complex developmental trajectories. Determining whether a child's development is typical or concerning requires age-appropriate clinical assessment, history, examination, and relevant testing by qualified professionals. A general description of a critical period or an average age range does not establish an individual diagnosis. Similarly, a parent's observation of a change in behavior or sensory function should be interpreted in context rather than attributed automatically to pruning or plasticity.
Study instruction:
- "Identify why population-level developmental research cannot replace individualized clinical assessment."
Key point:
- Developmental science explains general mechanisms, not individual diagnoses.
- Clinical concerns require qualified assessment and context-specific evidence.

58. Q: What are the implications of prolonged prefrontal development for understanding adolescent behavior?
A: Show answer
The chapter connects the prolonged refinement of the prefrontal cortex with the gradual improvement of executive functions. It suggests that adolescents may place considerable importance on peer approval and may engage in risk-taking to obtain it while prefrontal circuits are still maturing. This should not be interpreted as a universal description of all adolescents or as a reason to dismiss their judgment. Behavior is also influenced by experience, social context, temperament, and opportunity. The developmental perspective instead encourages adults to recognize that decision-making and reward evaluation continue to mature and that support, clear boundaries, and opportunities to practice judgment remain important.
Study instruction:

59. Q: How does the development of inhibitory circuits help stabilize sensory representations?
A: Show answer
Inhibitory interneurons regulate the activity of neighboring neurons and help shape how signals are processed within a cortical network. In the visual cortex described in the chapter, maturation of large basket cells refines inhibitory connections, while extracellular matrix structures help stabilize the emerging circuit. These changes reduce the system's susceptibility to large shifts caused by altered sensory input. Stabilization can be understood as consolidation of a circuit that has been shaped by experience. It is not simply the shutting down of activity; it is a change in how the network regulates and maintains its connections.

60. Q: Why is sensory experience described as a biological input rather than merely a source of information?
A: Show answer
The chapter demonstrates that sensory experience can influence the physical organization of neural circuits. Visual input affects which ocular-dominance connections are stabilized, and deprivation can alter the relative representation of the two eyes during a critical period. Experience therefore does more than provide information for an already formed brain to interpret; it participates in the developmental process that establishes the circuitry used to process that information. The effect depends on the state of the developing network and the biological mechanisms that regulate plasticity. Sensory experience is one component of development alongside genes and internal neural activity.

61. Q: What does the chapter teach about the relationship between growth and elimination in neural development?
A: Show answer
The chapter repeatedly shows that development requires both adding and removing biological structures. Neurons are initially overproduced, then some die when survival support is insufficient. Synapses are formed in excess and later pruned according to activity, experience, and trophic support. In the visual cortex, sensory input helps stabilize selected connections, while inhibitory maturation and extracellular matrix structures consolidate the emerging network. Growth without refinement would leave an overly redundant system; elimination without prior construction would leave too few options for circuit organization. The balance between these processes produces a more specialized and functional nervous system.
Study instruction:
- "Use examples from neuronal survival, synaptogenesis, and visual-cortex development to explain why growth and elimination are complementary."
Key point:
- Development is a cycle of construction, selection, and stabilization.
- Both growth and elimination are necessary for functional organization.

62. Q: How should the discovery history of NGF and the visual critical period be compared as examples of scientific progress?
A: Show answer
The NGF story began with observations about nerve growth toward transplanted tissues and developed through experiments that identified a diffusible survival factor. The visual critical-period story began with physiological recordings and experiments that manipulated visual input, revealing that sensory experience changes cortical organization during a limited developmental window. Both examples show how researchers moved from observation to testable hypotheses and experimental evidence. They also illustrate different kinds of evidence: biochemical identification and neutralization in the NGF work, and controlled sensory deprivation and cortical recording in the visual-system work. Together, they show that developmental plasticity is regulated by both molecular signals and patterned activity.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the experimental logic of the NGF discovery with the monocular-deprivation experiments of Hubel and Wiesel."
Key point:
- Developmental mechanisms are established through different but complementary experimental approaches.
- Molecular signaling and sensory activity both shape neural development.

63. Q: What limitations should be kept in mind when interpreting the chapter's numerical estimates about neurons and synapses?
A: Show answer
The chapter opens with approximate estimates of 86 to 100 billion neurons, a larger number of glial cells, and an order of magnitude of a quadrillion synaptic connections. These figures communicate the scale and complexity of the nervous system, but they are estimates rather than exact counts for every individual. Likewise, reported ages for synaptic peaks and critical periods describe findings from particular studies, species, regions, and methods. Numerical values are useful for illustrating magnitude and developmental trends, but they should not be treated as precise universal constants or diagnostic thresholds.

64. Q: What are the most important lessons from Chapter 3, “Developmental Plasticity”?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central lesson is that the developing nervous system is constructed through a combination of overproduction, selective survival, synapse formation, activity-dependent refinement, and stabilization. Neurotrophic factors such as NGF help determine which neurons survive and mature. Programmed cell death removes cells that do not receive adequate support. Immature neurons form more synapses than they retain, and electrical activity and experience help stabilize selected connections. Synaptic pruning continues through adolescence and into early adulthood in some regions, while adult brains continue to remodel synapses. Sensory experience shapes developing pathways during critical periods, and inhibitory circuits help regulate when these windows open and close. Development is therefore neither entirely predetermined nor infinitely malleable.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter by linking trophic support, programmed cell death, synaptogenesis, pruning, critical periods, and the limits of plasticity."
Key point:
- Development is an interaction of genetic programs, trophic signals, activity, and experience.
- Neural circuits are built and refined over an extended period, with plasticity continuing beyond childhood.
