Table 7
Nerve Injury and Brain Damage: Cortical Remapping, Phantom Sensations, Stroke Recovery, and Rehabilitation
1. Q: What is the central argument of the chapter on nerve injury and brain damage?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that injury changes neural pathways and can reorganize activity in the brain and spinal cord for months or years. These changes are not automatically restorative. After peripheral nerve injury or amputation, remapping may fail to restore lost function and can contribute to neuropathic pain or phantom sensations. After stroke, surviving networks may recruit alternative pathways that help compensate for damaged connections. The same broad capacity for plasticity can therefore produce different outcomes depending on the lesion, the pathways that remain, the timing of change, and the way the nervous system is subsequently used. The chapter's main distinction is between potentially maladaptive reorganization and compensatory reorganization that can support recovery.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the chapter by contrasting one maladaptive response to peripheral injury with one compensatory response after stroke."
Key point:
- Injury-induced plasticity can be harmful, helpful, or mixed.
- Functional outcome depends on the type of injury and the circuits available for reorganization.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
2. Q: Why is neuroplasticity after injury not necessarily beneficial?
A: Show answer
Neuroplasticity describes changes in neural connections, activity, or functional organization; it does not specify whether those changes improve a person's abilities. When normal input is lost, neighboring representations may expand into deprived cortical territory, but that reorganization may not recreate the original pathway or restore precise function. Altered peripheral signals, spinal excitability, and changes in cortical processing can instead contribute to abnormal sensations or persistent pain. After stroke, by contrast, surviving pathways may be strengthened or recruited to compensate for damage. Even then, compensation may produce less efficient or less precise movements. Plasticity should therefore be evaluated by its functional consequences, not assumed to be good simply because the brain has changed.
Study instruction:
- "For two injury examples, identify the neural change and then assess whether the resulting behavior is restorative, maladaptive, or both."
Key point:
- Plasticity is a mechanism of change, not a guarantee of recovery.
- The same reorganization can have different consequences in different contexts.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
- Relationship Between Chronic Pain and Brain Reorganization After Deafferentation
3. Q: What is a topographic map in the primary somatosensory and motor cortices?
A: Show answer
A topographic map is an organized representation in which neighboring parts of the body are represented in neighboring regions of cortex. In the primary somatosensory cortex, inputs from adjacent skin regions tend to be processed in adjacent cortical areas. In the primary motor cortex, neighboring groups of muscles are represented in nearby cortical regions that contribute to movement control. The maps are not literal miniature pictures of the body. They are functional arrangements of neural populations, and their boundaries can shift when input or use changes. This organization allows the brain to relate patterns of sensory input and motor output to specific parts of the body, while remaining capable of modification after injury or experience.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a simplified body map and explain how cortical adjacency differs from physical body size or shape."
Key point:
- Somatosensory and motor cortices contain organized body representations.
- These maps are functional and can be reorganized by altered input or use.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
4. Q: Why do the hands and face occupy disproportionately large cortical representations?
A: Show answer
The size of a cortical representation is related more closely to the density of sensory innervation or the complexity of motor control than to the physical dimensions of the body part. The hands contain many sensory receptors and support highly differentiated movements, while the face is also richly innervated and performs complex sensory and motor functions. As a result, substantial cortical territory is devoted to processing input from and controlling these regions. A cortical body map should therefore not be interpreted as a scale drawing of anatomical size. The disproportion reflects the functional demands placed on the nervous system by different body regions.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a small but highly sensitive body part can occupy more cortical territory than a physically larger region."
Key point:
- Cortical representation reflects neural and functional demands rather than body size alone.
- Hands and face receive large representations because of their sensory and motor importance.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
5. Q: What is cortical remapping, and what kinds of changes can produce it?
A: Show answer
Cortical remapping is a change in the functional territory or responsiveness of cortical areas representing particular body parts. If sensory input from one region is reduced, the deprived cortical area may become responsive to neighboring inputs. If a body part is used more intensively, its representation may expand. Injury can trigger remapping by removing normal signals, changing activity in surviving pathways, and allowing previously weak or silent connections to influence cortical responses. Remapping may occur quickly through changes in the effectiveness of existing connections and more gradually through structural growth and synaptic remodeling. It is therefore a family of processes rather than one single anatomical event.
Study instruction:
- "Compare deprivation-related shrinkage with use-related expansion and identify how each could change the balance of cortical representations."
Key point:
- Cortical maps can change when input or use changes.
- Rapid functional shifts and slower structural remodeling may both contribute.
Source links:
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
6. Q: What did studies of median-nerve injury in monkeys show about sensory cortical reorganization?
A: Show answer
When researchers severed the median nerve in a monkey's arm, the corresponding region of primary somatosensory cortex lost much of its usual input. Rather than remaining permanently silent, the deprived area gradually began responding to inputs from neighboring body regions. Cortical patches representing adjacent areas expanded into the territory that had previously represented the affected hand region. The experiment provided early direct evidence that adult sensory cortex can reorganize after peripheral nerve injury. It also showed that loss of input does not necessarily erase a cortical area; the area can become influenced by other pathways. The findings establish remapping, but they do not by themselves show that the new representation restores normal sensation or function.
Study instruction:
- "State the nerve manipulation, the cortical region affected, and the observed change in neighboring representations."
Key point:
- Deprived sensory cortex can be recruited by neighboring inputs.
- Demonstrating remapping does not prove that normal function has been restored.
Source links:
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
7. Q: What did rat sciatic-nerve experiments reveal about the speed and persistence of cortical remapping?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes experiments in which cutting the sciatic nerve led to a marked expansion of the somatosensory area normally devoted to input from the neighboring saphenous nerve. The change began within one to two days and could persist for as long as six months in the reported model. This demonstrates that cortical reorganization can begin rapidly after loss of peripheral input and remain detectable for an extended period. The result also shows why the timing of measurement matters: early changes may reflect altered effectiveness of existing connections, whereas longer-term changes can involve additional structural mechanisms. The exact time course should not be generalized to every nerve, species, or human injury.
Study instruction:
- "Place the onset and persistence of the rat remapping result on a timeline and identify why model-specific timing matters."
Key point:
- Peripheral injury can trigger cortical changes within days.
- The duration and magnitude of remapping vary by model and injury.
Source links:
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
8. Q: How does cortical reorganization after amputation relate to phantom-limb sensations?
A: Show answer
After an arm or hand is amputated, the cortical territory that previously received input from the missing limb loses its normal peripheral signals. The chapter explains that neighboring representations, especially the face representation located close to the hand representation, can expand into the deprived area. As a result, touching parts of an amputee's face may sometimes evoke sensations referred to the missing limb. The phenomenon suggests that the brain's representation of the missing body part can remain functionally accessible even after the limb is gone. Phantom sensations are not imaginary or deliberately produced; they are experiences associated with ongoing neural activity and changes in the nervous system after amputation.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the proposed relationship between adjacent face and hand representations and a touch-evoked phantom sensation."
Key point:
- Phantom sensations can involve activity in neural systems that previously represented the missing limb.
- Cortical remapping is one proposed contributor, not a complete explanation of every phantom experience.
Source links:
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
- Phantom Pain Is Associated With Preserved Structure and Function in the Former Hand Area
9. Q: Is cortical invasion by neighboring representations a complete explanation of phantom-limb pain?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter presents cortical remapping as one explanation for phantom sensations, but later research shows that the relationship between cortical reorganization and phantom pain is more complex than a simple story of neighboring areas taking over the missing limb's territory. Some studies find associations between remapping and pain, while others find that phantom pain can coexist with preserved structure or activity in the former hand area. Peripheral nerve activity, spinal mechanisms, ongoing sensory experience, attention, and other brain networks may also contribute. The appropriate conclusion is that remapping is relevant but not sufficient as a universal explanation. Phantom pain should be understood as a complex condition rather than attributed to one cortical map change.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the original remapping account with later findings that challenge a simple one-to-one relationship between remapping and pain."
Key point:
- Phantom pain has multiple interacting neural and experiential contributors.
- Cortical remapping is important evidence but not a complete causal model.
Source links:
- Relationship Between Chronic Pain and Brain Reorganization After Deafferentation
- Phantom Pain Is Associated With Preserved Structure and Function in the Former Hand Area
10. Q: What did facial-nerve injury experiments reveal about reorganization of the motor cortex?
A: Show answer
In the rat experiments described in the chapter, the facial nerve normally controls whisker movements. After the nerve was severed, the motor cortical region associated with the whiskers initially became unresponsive to electrical stimulation. A few hours later, stimulation of that region produced contractions in other muscles, including forearm and eyelid muscles. The result showed that motor cortical output can change rapidly after peripheral injury and that other motor pathways may become functionally accessible. It does not mean that the cortex has instantly grown a new set of anatomical connections. Rapid changes can arise when existing pathways become more influential or are no longer suppressed by the previous pattern of input and output.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish rapid changes in motor output from slower structural growth, using the facial-nerve experiment."
Key point:
- Motor cortical responses can be reorganized rapidly after peripheral nerve injury.
- A changed response does not automatically imply new anatomical wiring.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
11. Q: What does temporary nerve blockade reveal about the speed and reversibility of cortical remapping?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes human studies using a local anesthetic to temporarily block nerve input. During the block, the motor cortical area corresponding to the affected body region became less responsive, while neighboring areas increased their output to spinal motor neurons. The changes appeared quickly and disappeared about twenty minutes after the anesthetic effect wore off. This demonstrates that some cortical reorganization can occur through rapid changes in the effectiveness of existing pathways and can reverse when normal input returns. It also cautions against interpreting every change in a cortical map as permanent structural rewiring. Temporary changes in input can alter cortical responses without producing lasting anatomical changes.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the rapid reversible effect of temporary nerve blockade with the longer-lasting remapping observed after amputation."
Key point:
- Some cortical changes occur quickly and reverse when normal input returns.
- Functional remapping does not always mean permanent structural reorganization.
Source links:
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
12. Q: How do transcranial magnetic stimulation and transcranial direct current stimulation help researchers study cortical organization?
A: Show answer
Transcranial magnetic stimulation, or TMS, uses a coil to generate a brief magnetic field that induces an electric field in targeted brain tissue. Depending on the protocol, it can evoke or modulate activity in a cortical region and help map motor responses or test the contribution of a region to a task. Transcranial direct current stimulation, or tDCS, applies low-amplitude direct current through scalp electrodes and can alter cortical excitability. Both techniques can be used alongside behavioral measures and neuroimaging to investigate changes in brain function. They do not provide a direct microscopic picture of every synapse, and their effects depend on stimulation parameters, anatomy, task, and individual physiology.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the physical principle of TMS with tDCS and state what each can reveal about cortical function."
Key point:
- TMS and tDCS are noninvasive methods for probing or modulating brain activity.
- They measure or influence network function indirectly rather than directly visualizing individual synapses.
Source links:
- A Translational Roadmap for Transcranial Magnetic and Direct Current Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
- tDCS for Activities of Daily Living and Functioning After Stroke
13. Q: What cellular changes may occur in neurons after peripheral nerve injury?
A: Show answer
Animal studies described in the chapter suggest that neurons deprived of normal input can retract dendritic branches and lose some of the synaptic terminals that previously contacted them. At the injury site, intact axons may sprout new branches that grow into the damaged region. These changes alter the balance of inputs a neuron receives and the targets contacted by surviving fibers. They provide a possible cellular basis for the larger-scale remapping observed in cortical recordings. However, imaging methods used in living humans are not sensitive enough to identify every microscopic event directly, so some mechanistic interpretations are based on animal experiments and must be treated as evidence-informed models rather than direct observation of all human injuries.
Study instruction:
- "Separate the cellular observations made in animal models from the cortical responses measured in human studies."
Key point:
- Injury can change dendrites, synaptic input, and axonal branching.
- Human cortical mapping does not directly reveal every microscopic mechanism.
Source links:
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
- Relationship Between Chronic Pain and Brain Reorganization After Deafferentation
14. Q: How can axonal sprouting after injury contribute to either recovery or maladaptive signaling?
A: Show answer
Axonal sprouting occurs when surviving nerve fibers extend new branches. If those branches establish appropriate connections, they may help restore communication or provide alternative routes around damaged tissue. If they connect with inappropriate targets or become excessively active, they may produce abnormal sensory or motor signals. The chapter notes that sprouting at an injury site can create mismatches between fibers and their new targets, potentially contributing to neuropathic pain. The biological process itself is not inherently beneficial or harmful; its consequences depend on where the new branches grow, what cells they contact, and how the resulting pathway functions. Successful repair requires appropriate targeting and functional integration, not growth alone.
Study instruction:
- "Construct one example in which axonal sprouting could support recovery and one in which it could produce abnormal signaling."
Key point:
- Axonal growth can create alternative connections after injury.
- The accuracy and function of new connections determine their consequences.
Source links:
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
15. Q: What is unmasking of previously silent neural connections?
A: Show answer
Unmasking refers to the increased functional influence of connections that already exist but normally contribute little to the observed response. After injury, the loss of dominant input or changes in inhibition can allow these previously weak pathways to become more effective. The chapter discusses horizontal connections between neighboring cortical areas and vertical connections involving the thalamus as possible contributors to early remapping. Unmasking can produce rapid changes because it does not require the growth of entirely new axons before a different response becomes visible. It is one proposed early mechanism of reorganization, and it can be followed by more lasting structural changes as surviving pathways strengthen and new branches form.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why unmasking can alter cortical responses faster than the growth of new axonal branches."
Key point:
- Existing but weak connections can become functionally influential after injury.
- Unmasking is a proposed early mechanism that may precede structural remodeling.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
16. Q: How can long-term potentiation and structural remodeling help consolidate cortical reorganization?
A: Show answer
Long-term potentiation, or LTP, is a lasting increase in synaptic effectiveness after particular patterns of activity. The chapter proposes that unmasked connections can become stronger through LTP, while longer-lasting changes may be consolidated by axonal sprouting, dendritic growth, and formation of new synapses. These mechanisms can help turn a transient change in responsiveness into a more persistent pattern of connectivity. LTP is not the only mechanism involved, and it does not guarantee that the resulting connection is useful. The functional value of consolidation depends on the pathway recruited and the tasks in which it is used. Rehabilitation can influence which surviving pathways are repeatedly activated and strengthened.
Study instruction:
- "Describe the difference between a short-term functional shift, LTP-related strengthening, and later structural remodeling."
Key point:
- LTP can strengthen existing synaptic connections.
- Structural growth may help stabilize longer-lasting reorganization.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
17. Q: Why can peripheral nerve injury lead to neuropathic pain rather than functional recovery?
A: Show answer
Peripheral nerve injury can remove normal sensory input while also changing activity in damaged fibers, surviving axons, spinal circuits, and cortical representations. The resulting signals may no longer correspond to the original stimulus or body location. Sprouting fibers may contact mismatched targets, and previously weak pathways may become more influential. Such changes can generate abnormal sensory experiences rather than restore accurate sensation. The chapter emphasizes that remapping after nerve injury and amputation often does not produce significant functional recovery, particularly after serious injury. Neuropathic pain is complex and cannot be attributed to cortical remapping alone, but altered neural organization is one part of the broader mechanism.
Study instruction:
- "Identify at least three levels of the nervous system that may contribute to neuropathic pain after a peripheral injury."
Key point:
- Abnormal input and reorganization can produce persistent pain or altered sensation.
- Neuropathic pain involves multiple interacting mechanisms, not one cortical change.
Source links:
- Relationship Between Chronic Pain and Brain Reorganization After Deafferentation
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
18. Q: What is a stroke, and how does it cause brain injury?
A: Show answer
A stroke occurs when blood supply to part of the brain is interrupted by a blocked vessel or a ruptured vessel. The affected tissue receives insufficient oxygen and other essential resources, which can cause brain cells to die. The location and extent of the injury determine which functions are disrupted. Damage involving motor pathways can produce weakness or paralysis, while injury to language-related regions can cause speech or comprehension difficulties. Stroke is therefore not one uniform condition: different vascular events affect different networks and produce different deficits. The chapter focuses on the subsequent reorganization of surviving pathways, but acute stroke is a medical emergency requiring immediate professional assessment and treatment.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish ischemic and hemorrhagic mechanisms at a high level and connect lesion location with the type of deficit."
Key point:
- Stroke damages brain tissue by disrupting its blood supply.
- The effects depend strongly on which neural networks are injured.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
19. Q: Why can a stroke in one cerebral hemisphere affect movement on the opposite side of the body?
A: Show answer
Many descending motor pathways cross from one side of the nervous system to the other before reaching the muscles they control. Consequently, damage to motor regions in the left hemisphere commonly affects movement on the right side of the body, and damage to the right hemisphere commonly affects the left side. The chapter uses this organization to explain the contralateral weakness or paralysis that can follow stroke. The exact pattern depends on the lesion's location, size, and the pathways affected; not every stroke produces the same deficit. Understanding the crossing of motor pathways helps clinicians interpret symptoms and identify which neural systems may need to be assessed during rehabilitation.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the major left-to-right and right-to-left relationships described in the chapter and use them to explain contralateral weakness."
Key point:
- Many motor pathways cross, producing opposite-side effects from hemispheric injury.
- Lesion location determines the particular movement deficits that occur.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
20. Q: How can surviving brain pathways compensate for motor pathways damaged by stroke?
A: Show answer
After stroke, the brain may recruit alternative pathways that run alongside or around the damaged descending motor route. These pathways can originate from secondary motor regions near the lesion or from motor areas in the opposite hemisphere. By strengthening or increasing the use of surviving connections, the nervous system can re-establish communication with spinal motor neurons and muscles. The new route is often less direct than the original one, but it can support useful movement. This spontaneous compensation is one reason some patients improve even without complete restoration of the damaged tissue. Rehabilitation can help reinforce the pathways that are capable of supporting functional movement.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a damaged direct pathway and one alternative route, then explain how repeated practice could strengthen the surviving route."
Key point:
- Surviving pathways can provide alternative routes for motor commands.
- Recovery often depends on strengthening networks that were not destroyed by the stroke.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
21. Q: What is the difference between ipsilesional and contralesional contributions to stroke recovery?
A: Show answer
Ipsilesional refers to the hemisphere on the same side as the brain lesion, while contralesional refers to the hemisphere on the opposite side. After stroke, surviving regions near the lesion in the affected hemisphere may help reorganize motor output, and the opposite hemisphere may also contribute through alternative pathways. The relative importance of these regions depends on the severity and location of the injury and on the patient's remaining connections. It is therefore too simple to say that one hemisphere always helps and the other always interferes. Functional imaging and stimulation studies can help determine which networks are supporting a particular patient's movement, but their findings must be interpreted in context.
Study instruction:
- "Label ipsilesional and contralesional hemispheres in a diagram and describe one possible contribution from each."
Key point:
- Both hemispheres may contribute to post-stroke reorganization.
- The value of each contribution depends on lesion characteristics and recovery stage.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
- A Translational Roadmap for Brain Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
22. Q: Why may compensatory motor pathways restore gross movement but not fine finger control?
A: Show answer
The original motor pathway from primary motor cortex to spinal motor neurons can provide relatively direct and differentiated control. Alternative pathways recruited after stroke often involve additional synapses and may activate groups of muscles together rather than individual muscles or fibers. This can allow a patient to regain a broad movement, such as reaching or grasping, while still struggling with isolated finger movements or precise coordination. The recovery is real but incomplete because the new route does not reproduce every property of the damaged pathway. Rehabilitation therefore needs to target the specific functions that remain impaired, rather than judging progress only by whether a limb can move at all.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a direct motor pathway with a more indirect compensatory route and explain why the latter may produce less precise movement."
Key point:
- Alternative pathways can restore useful movement without restoring normal precision.
- Fine motor control may remain impaired even when gross movement improves.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
23. Q: What structural and sensory changes can occur in the somatosensory cortex after stroke?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes imaging studies in chronic stroke patients that found increases in cortical thickness in parts of the primary somatosensory cortex. These structural changes were associated with stronger cortical responses to touch and heightened touch sensitivity compared with control participants. The findings suggest that stroke can alter not only motor networks but also sensory processing in surviving cortical tissue. However, an association between cortical thickness and sensory response does not establish that one directly causes the other or that increased thickness always indicates better function. Structural and functional measures need to be interpreted alongside clinical performance and the details of the injury.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why structural imaging and functional sensory testing provide complementary rather than interchangeable evidence."
Key point:
- Stroke can produce long-term structural and functional changes in sensory cortex.
- A measurable cortical change does not automatically mean improved function.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
24. Q: Why does recovery from stroke involve relearning movement rather than simply waiting for the original pathway to return?
A: Show answer
When the original motor pathway is damaged, surviving networks may have to support movement through alternative routes. Those routes are often less efficient and may activate muscles in unfamiliar combinations. The patient therefore needs to practice movements with the reorganized system so that the new pathways become more effective and coordinated. Rehabilitation is a form of motor relearning: it repeatedly engages the affected limb and provides feedback about the accuracy and usefulness of the movement. Recovery is not merely spontaneous repair of the damaged tissue. It involves learning to use the neural resources that remain available, while also addressing secondary problems such as weakness, reduced coordination, and limited confidence in movement.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how repeated movement practice can help a patient learn to use alternative neural pathways after stroke."
Key point:
- Recovery often involves learning to use reorganized pathways.
- Rehabilitation helps strengthen and refine the movements that surviving networks can support.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
- National Clinical Guideline for Stroke: Motor Recovery and Rehabilitation
25. Q: Why is repetitive physiotherapy important after stroke?
A: Show answer
Repetitive physiotherapy repeatedly activates the movements and neural pathways that a patient is trying to recover. Practice provides opportunities for surviving circuits to strengthen, coordinate, and become more efficient. The chapter emphasizes the importance of intensive movement practice in the months following stroke, particularly for the affected limb. Repetition alone is not enough if the task is inappropriate or the movement is unsafe; practice should be tailored to the patient's abilities and functional goals. Feedback, progression, rest, and consistency help make repetition useful. Rehabilitation also needs to address motivation and access, because a technically sound exercise program cannot produce its intended effect if the patient cannot carry it out.
Study instruction:
- "Design a simple task-practice example and specify the target movement, repetition, feedback, progression, and safety considerations."
Key point:
- Repeated task practice helps engage and strengthen surviving motor pathways.
- Practice must be individualized and safe to be effective.
Source links:
- National Clinical Guideline for Stroke: Motor Recovery and Rehabilitation
- Repetitive Task Practice: A Critical Review of Constraint-Induced Movement Therapy in Stroke
26. Q: What is constraint-induced movement therapy, and what is its intended mechanism?
A: Show answer
Constraint-induced movement therapy encourages use of the weakened limb by limiting use of the less-affected limb for selected periods and combining this constraint with intensive, task-oriented practice of the affected limb. The approach is intended to counter learned non-use, in which a patient relies increasingly on the stronger limb because attempts with the affected limb are difficult or unsuccessful. Repeated practice gives the affected limb more opportunities to improve and helps translate movement gains into everyday tasks. The method is not appropriate for every patient; sufficient voluntary movement, medical stability, safety, and professional assessment matter. Modified protocols may reduce the duration of constraint and adapt practice to individual ability.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how constraint and repetitive practice work together, and identify patient factors that affect suitability."
Key point:
- Constraint-induced therapy promotes active use of the affected limb through structured practice.
- It requires appropriate patient selection and adaptation to functional ability.
Source links:
- Repetitive Task Practice: A Critical Review of Constraint-Induced Movement Therapy in Stroke
- National Clinical Guideline for Stroke: Motor Recovery and Rehabilitation
27. Q: Why has robotic technology become increasingly relevant to stroke rehabilitation?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that intensive rehabilitation can be difficult to deliver because patients may struggle to maintain motivation and physiotherapists may be in short supply. Robotic systems can help deliver repeated, measurable movement practice and may reduce some of the physical demands on therapists. They can also provide consistent assistance and track performance over time. However, a robot does not replace clinical judgment, individualized goal-setting, or the social and motivational aspects of rehabilitation. Technology is useful when it supports meaningful practice and is integrated into a broader treatment plan. The value of robotic rehabilitation should be assessed by functional outcomes, not simply by the number of repetitions or the sophistication of the device.
Study instruction:
- "Compare therapist-led and robot-assisted practice in terms of repetition, feedback, personalization, access, and real-world transfer."
Key point:
- Robotics can support intensive and measurable rehabilitation practice.
- Technology should complement, not replace, individualized clinical care.
Source links:
- National Clinical Guideline for Stroke: Motor Recovery and Rehabilitation
28. Q: Why do stroke patients show substantial differences in recovery outcomes?
A: Show answer
Recovery varies because strokes differ in location, size, mechanism, and the amount of neural tissue and connectivity that remain. Patients also differ in age, general health, genetic background, prior abilities, complications, access to rehabilitation, motivation, and the timing and intensity of treatment. The chapter describes broad groups of patients with large, moderate, or minimal improvement, but those proportions should be treated as a summary from the source rather than a precise forecast for an individual. A population-level statistic cannot determine what will happen to a particular patient. Recovery assessment requires repeated clinical evaluation and attention to the specific functions and pathways affected.
Study instruction:
- "List biological, injury-related, and environmental factors that can contribute to variation in stroke recovery."
Key point:
- Recovery is highly individual and depends on multiple interacting factors.
- Group averages should not be used as deterministic predictions for one patient.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
29. Q: Why do the timing of stroke diagnosis and the start of rehabilitation matter?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes that rapid diagnosis and treatment can limit the amount of brain tissue damaged by interrupted blood supply. It also notes that earlier rehabilitation is generally associated with better outcomes because surviving pathways can be engaged while the nervous system is adapting. These are related but distinct time-sensitive issues. Acute stroke treatment is intended to address the immediate vascular emergency and must follow medical protocols, whereas rehabilitation is planned after clinical assessment and stabilization. The correct timing and intensity depend on the patient's condition. The chapter's broad principle is that both prompt medical care and appropriately timed rehabilitation influence the opportunities for recovery.
Study instruction:
- "Separate acute stroke treatment from post-stroke rehabilitation and explain why each has its own timing and clinical criteria."
Key point:
- Rapid treatment can limit the initial injury caused by stroke.
- Timely, appropriately planned rehabilitation can support recovery of surviving pathways.
Source links:
- National Clinical Guideline for Stroke: Motor Recovery and Rehabilitation
30. Q: What is interhemispheric inhibition, and why does it matter after stroke?
A: Show answer
The two cerebral hemispheres communicate through pathways including fibers in the corpus callosum, and their activity can influence or inhibit activity in the opposite hemisphere. This reciprocal interaction helps coordinate movement and maintain an appropriate balance of activity. After stroke, the damaged hemisphere may become less able to exert its normal influence, while the unaffected hemisphere may become relatively more active. Depending on the stage and severity of injury, this imbalance may help recruit alternative pathways or may interfere with the recovery of the damaged side. The chapter therefore treats interhemispheric balance as a possible factor in recovery, not as a simple rule that one hemisphere should always be suppressed.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a lesion can alter the balance of activity between hemispheres and why the effect may change over time."
Key point:
- The hemispheres influence one another through reciprocal connections.
- The effect of interhemispheric imbalance depends on lesion and recovery stage.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
- A Translational Roadmap for Brain Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
31. Q: How can TMS be used to influence motor recovery after stroke?
A: Show answer
TMS can deliver brief magnetic pulses that induce electrical currents in targeted cortical tissue. Depending on the stimulation protocol, it can increase or reduce the excitability of a selected region and can be used to test the contribution of that region to movement. Researchers have investigated whether modifying activity in one hemisphere can improve the balance of motor-network activity after stroke. The chapter describes early studies in which suppressing activity on the unaffected side appeared to help some patients, while results in others were different. TMS is therefore a potential adjunct and research tool, not a universal treatment. Its effects depend on the patient, timing, stimulation parameters, and the network state being targeted.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how a magnetic pulse can influence cortical activity and identify why the same protocol may not help every patient."
Key point:
- TMS can probe and modulate cortical excitability.
- Clinical effects depend on individualized targeting and recovery stage.
Source links:
- A Translational Roadmap for Transcranial Magnetic and Direct Current Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
32. Q: Why can the timing of hemispheric stimulation change whether TMS helps or hinders recovery?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes evidence that the relationship between the hemispheres may change during recovery. Early after stroke, the unaffected hemisphere may become overactive partly because the damaged hemisphere provides less inhibition. At another stage, the hemispheres may shift from an inhibitory relationship toward one that is more supportive or excitatory. A stimulation strategy that is useful in the first state could therefore be ineffective or counterproductive after the network relationship changes. This is one reason stimulation should be guided by assessment rather than applied according to a fixed rule. The underlying goal is to support the network configuration that best contributes to functional recovery at that time.
Study instruction:
- "Create a two-stage model showing how an intervention could have different effects before and after a change in interhemispheric interactions."
Key point:
- Brain-network relationships can evolve during post-stroke recovery.
- Neuromodulation must account for timing and individual network state.
Source links:
- A Translational Roadmap for Transcranial Magnetic and Direct Current Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
33. Q: How does tDCS differ from TMS in the way it influences the brain?
A: Show answer
TMS uses a changing magnetic field to induce a brief electric field in targeted brain tissue. tDCS instead passes a low-amplitude direct current between electrodes placed on the scalp, changing the excitability of neural tissue under and around the electrodes. Both methods can influence brain activity, but their physical mechanisms, spatial effects, and stimulation protocols differ. The chapter discusses tDCS as a method for modulating cortical activity and potentially supporting plasticity during rehabilitation. Neither method directly repairs a damaged blood vessel or replaces lost neurons. Their usefulness depends on how stimulation is combined with meaningful rehabilitation and whether the resulting changes improve real-world function.
Study instruction:
- "Compare TMS and tDCS by energy source, stimulation pattern, and the kind of neural effect each is intended to produce."
Key point:
- TMS uses magnetic pulses; tDCS uses low-amplitude direct current.
- Both modulate brain activity and require evidence-based clinical application.
Source links:
- tDCS for Activities of Daily Living and Functioning After Stroke
- A Translational Roadmap for Transcranial Magnetic and Direct Current Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
34. Q: What are the limitations of using tDCS as an adjunct to stroke rehabilitation?
A: Show answer
tDCS can alter cortical excitability, but that does not guarantee a meaningful improvement in movement or independence. Systematic reviews have found uncertainty about its effectiveness for several stroke outcomes, with evidence quality varying across measures and studies. Results can depend on electrode placement, stimulation dose, patient characteristics, timing, and the rehabilitation activities performed alongside stimulation. Some reviews report possible benefits for selected activities of daily living, while evidence for consistent improvement in arm function, leg function, strength, or cognition remains limited. tDCS should therefore be considered an adjunct under appropriate clinical oversight, not a substitute for established rehabilitation or a guaranteed recovery method.
Study instruction:
- "Identify at least three methodological or clinical factors that can explain why tDCS trial results may differ."
Key point:
- Modulating cortical excitability does not automatically produce functional improvement.
- Current evidence supports cautious, outcome-specific interpretation of tDCS research.
Source links:
- Cochrane Review: tDCS for Functioning After Stroke
- A Translational Roadmap for Transcranial Magnetic and Direct Current Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
35. Q: How can functional neuroimaging and TMS help identify brain regions that support post-stroke movement?
A: Show answer
Functional MRI can show which regions become more active when a patient performs a movement, while TMS can test how disrupting or stimulating a region affects performance. The chapter describes findings in which secondary motor regions near the damaged hemisphere became more active as movement impairment increased. TMS disruption of these regions impaired movement in stroke patients, suggesting that they had become functionally important for the reorganized motor system. Other findings showed that severely affected patients could be more dependent on secondary motor areas in the unaffected hemisphere. These methods provide complementary evidence about network involvement, but activation alone does not prove that a region is essential or that its increased activity is beneficial.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how fMRI and TMS answer different questions about a secondary motor region after stroke."
Key point:
- Imaging identifies activity patterns, while TMS can test causal contribution more directly.
- Increased activity may reflect compensation, difficulty, or altered network recruitment.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
- A Translational Roadmap for Brain Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
36. Q: Why can stroke cause language deficits, and how may language networks reorganize?
A: Show answer
In many people, language functions are strongly lateralized to regions of the left frontal and temporal lobes. A stroke that damages these regions or their connections can cause difficulties producing or understanding language. The chapter describes compensatory plasticity in which surrounding areas of the damaged left hemisphere, language-related regions in the right hemisphere, or both may become more involved. The extent and usefulness of this recruitment depend on lesion location, remaining network connections, age, and the type of language impairment. Reorganization may support partial recovery, but it does not guarantee a return to normal language function. Language rehabilitation therefore requires assessment of the specific abilities that have been affected.
Study instruction:
- "Map the major possibilities for language-network recruitment after a left-hemisphere stroke and distinguish them from guaranteed restoration."
Key point:
- Language is usually left-lateralized, but both hemispheres can contribute after injury.
- Recovery depends on the remaining network and the specific language functions affected.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
37. Q: Why have attempts to use hemispheric stimulation to improve post-stroke language recovery produced mixed results?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that language recovery may involve recruitment of surrounding regions in the damaged left hemisphere, regions in the right hemisphere, or both. Because language is usually left-lateralized, researchers have investigated whether changing the balance of activity between hemispheres could improve recovery. However, the optimal balance may vary by patient, lesion, stage of recovery, and the particular language task. Suppressing activity in one region could remove a source of interference in one patient but suppress a useful compensatory pathway in another. The chapter therefore presents the approach as an early research direction with conflicting results, not as an established universal intervention.
Study instruction:
- "Give one reason why suppressing a hemisphere might help in one patient but hinder another during language rehabilitation."
Key point:
- Language-network reorganization is highly individual.
- Stimulation strategies require patient-specific evidence and careful evaluation.
Source links:
- A Translational Roadmap for Brain Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
38. Q: What did the chapter report about fluoxetine and motor recovery after stroke?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an early clinical study in which patients receiving fluoxetine alongside physiotherapy showed greater motor improvement over a three-month period than patients receiving placebo. At the time, possible explanations included anti-inflammatory effects and facilitation of plasticity in newly recruited motor pathways. The chapter also states that the mechanism was uncertain. Later, larger randomized trials did not support routine fluoxetine use to improve functional recovery after stroke and reported important safety concerns, including increased fractures in some studies. The early result should therefore be understood as a promising initial finding that was not confirmed as a general treatment strategy. Medication decisions must be made by qualified clinicians for approved indications and individual circumstances.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the early FLAME trial finding with later larger trials and explain why a promising small study is not sufficient for routine treatment."
Key point:
- Early evidence suggested possible benefit, but later large trials did not confirm routine functional benefit.
- A mechanistic hypothesis is not a substitute for replicated clinical evidence.
Source links:
- FLAME: Fluoxetine for Motor Recovery After Acute Ischaemic Stroke
- FOCUS: Fluoxetine and Functional Outcomes After Acute Stroke
- EFFECTS: Safety and Efficacy of Fluoxetine After Acute Stroke
39. Q: Why is it important to distinguish an early positive clinical trial from later confirmatory evidence?
A: Show answer
Early trials may be relatively small and can produce encouraging results that later studies do not reproduce. Larger, well-designed trials can provide more precise estimates of benefits and harms across broader patient populations. The chapter's discussion of fluoxetine illustrates this issue: an early study reported improved motor scores when fluoxetine was combined with physiotherapy, but later large trials found no meaningful improvement in overall functional outcome and reported increased risks such as fractures or hyponatremia. The evidence therefore changed as more data became available. Responsible clinical interpretation requires considering trial size, design, outcomes, replication, adverse effects, and whether results apply to the patient group under consideration.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a clinical claim by comparing trial design, sample size, primary outcome, replication, and adverse events."
Key point:
- Initial positive findings need confirmation in larger and independent studies.
- Clinical recommendations must weigh benefits against harms and uncertainty.
Source links:
- FOCUS: Fluoxetine and Functional Outcomes After Acute Stroke
- AFFINITY: Fluoxetine and Functional Outcome After Acute Stroke
- EFFECTS: Safety and Efficacy of Fluoxetine After Acute Stroke
40. Q: What is the difference between true motor recovery and compensation after stroke?
A: Show answer
True recovery means that the nervous system regains the ability to produce a movement pattern that resembles the original function, using the relevant muscles and control strategy. Compensation means that a person achieves a task goal through a different movement pattern or alternative muscles and pathways. Both can improve independence, but they are not identical outcomes. For example, a patient may regain the ability to reach for an object using a different combination of shoulder and trunk movements while still lacking normal hand control. Rehabilitation should measure both task success and movement quality so that improvement is not mistaken for full restoration of the original motor pattern.
Study instruction:
- "Classify two post-stroke movement examples as restoration or compensation and justify the classification."
Key point:
- Compensation can improve task performance without restoring the original movement pattern.
- Functional assessment should consider both success and movement quality.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
- National Clinical Guideline for Stroke: Motor Recovery and Rehabilitation
41. Q: What principles should guide the design of a rehabilitation program after stroke?
A: Show answer
A rehabilitation program should begin with assessment of the patient's deficits, abilities, medical stability, and personally meaningful goals. Practice should target specific tasks and movements that the patient needs to perform, with repetition and progression matched to capacity. Feedback helps the patient understand performance, while appropriate challenge encourages learning without creating unnecessary risk or fatigue. Programs may combine therapist-guided exercises, constraint-induced practice, assistive devices, and other methods according to the individual's condition. The chapter emphasizes that rehabilitation is not one-size-fits-all: severity, motivation, access to therapy, and the networks available for recruitment influence what is feasible and effective. Progress should be monitored and the plan adjusted as the patient changes.
Study instruction:
- "Create a rehabilitation plan for one functional goal and specify assessment, task practice, progression, feedback, safety, and review."
Key point:
- Rehabilitation should be individualized and linked to meaningful functional goals.
- Repetition, feedback, progression, and monitoring support effective practice.
Source links:
- National Clinical Guideline for Stroke: Motor Recovery and Rehabilitation
42. Q: What are the risks of treating TMS or tDCS as a stand-alone solution for stroke recovery?
A: Show answer
Brain stimulation can influence neural activity, but it cannot replace the need for medical care, task-specific rehabilitation, or appropriate assessment. A stimulation protocol may fail to help if it targets the wrong region, is delivered at the wrong stage, or is not combined with relevant practice. In some circumstances, altering activity in a compensatory region could disrupt a pathway that the patient relies on. Evidence for some outcomes remains variable, and clinical protocols are still being refined. Stimulation should therefore be used only within appropriate professional and research frameworks, with monitoring and realistic expectations about its potential contribution.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three ways in which neuromodulation could fail to produce functional improvement even if it changes cortical excitability."
Key point:
- A measurable neural effect does not guarantee a meaningful clinical benefit.
- Neuromodulation should complement individualized rehabilitation and evidence-based care.
Source links:
- A Translational Roadmap for Brain Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
- Cochrane Review: tDCS for Functioning After Stroke
43. Q: Why are animal models important for studying injury-induced plasticity, and what are their limitations?
A: Show answer
Animal models allow researchers to manipulate nerves, pathways, and activity under controlled conditions and then examine changes at cellular and circuit levels that are difficult to observe directly in humans. Rat barrel-cortex studies, monkey nerve-injury experiments, and other models have helped identify mechanisms of remapping and axonal change. However, species differ in anatomy, behavior, developmental timing, and the complexity of their sensory and motor tasks. A cortical change observed in a rat or monkey does not automatically predict the same functional outcome in a person. Human imaging and clinical studies are needed to test whether proposed mechanisms are relevant to patients and whether interventions improve meaningful outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "For one animal experiment, identify what it can establish directly and what additional human evidence would be needed."
Key point:
- Animal models reveal mechanisms under controlled experimental conditions.
- Translating findings to human rehabilitation requires additional clinical evidence.
Source links:
- Brain Reorganisation Following Amputation: Implications for Phantom Limb Pain
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
44. Q: Why should cortical remapping and behavioral recovery be measured separately?
A: Show answer
Cortical remapping describes changes in neural representation or activity, whereas behavioral recovery describes changes in what a person can feel, move, communicate, or do in daily life. A larger or more active cortical representation may reflect compensation, increased effort, altered inhibition, or maladaptive signaling rather than improved function. Conversely, a patient may improve through alternative pathways even when the original cortical map does not return to its previous form. Researchers therefore need both neural measurements and functional outcomes. A change in an imaging or stimulation measure is evidence of neural reorganization, but it is not by itself proof that the patient has recovered a lost ability.
Study instruction:
- "Design a simple study that measures both cortical organization and a meaningful functional outcome after injury."
Key point:
- Neural change and functional improvement are related but distinct outcomes.
- Rehabilitation success should be judged by patient-relevant abilities, not imaging alone.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
- Relationship Between Chronic Pain and Brain Reorganization After Deafferentation
45. Q: Why can a patient develop a plateau in recovery even while the brain remains plastic?
A: Show answer
Plasticity does not mean that every pathway can be restored indefinitely or that all forms of practice will produce further improvement. Recovery may plateau because the remaining pathways are limited, the original lesion was severe, the patient has reached the current capacity of a compensatory network, or practice no longer provides an appropriate challenge. Pain, fatigue, spasticity, cognitive impairment, depression, medical complications, and access barriers can also limit participation. A plateau should prompt reassessment of goals, task selection, and contributing factors rather than the assumption that the brain has become completely incapable of change. The chapter's central message is that plasticity creates opportunities, but those opportunities have biological and practical limits.
Study instruction:
- "List possible biological, behavioral, and environmental reasons for a recovery plateau and identify which require clinical reassessment."
Key point:
- Plasticity is constrained by lesion severity, network capacity, and patient circumstances.
- A plateau calls for reassessment rather than an assumption of zero remaining potential.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
46. Q: How do genetic and environmental factors influence the extent of recovery after stroke?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies both genetic and environmental factors as possible contributors to the wide variation in recovery. Genetic differences may influence neural repair, inflammation, plasticity, and other biological responses, while environmental factors include access to rehabilitation, social support, health conditions, motivation, and opportunities for repeated practice. The interaction between these factors means that two patients with similar lesions may experience different outcomes. The chapter does not identify a single genetic marker or environmental condition that determines recovery. The appropriate interpretation is that recovery is multifactorial and that biological potential must be considered alongside the circumstances in which rehabilitation takes place.
Study instruction:
- "Separate inherited biological influences from modifiable environmental influences in a case study of two patients with similar strokes."
Key point:
- Recovery reflects interacting biological and environmental factors.
- No single factor reliably determines the outcome for an individual patient.
Source links:
- Spasticity, Motor Recovery, and Neural Plasticity After Stroke
47. Q: How should broad recovery statistics from a chapter be used when discussing an individual patient's prognosis?
A: Show answer
Broad statistics can describe patterns observed in a study population, but they do not predict an individual's outcome with certainty. The chapter gives approximate proportions of patients with substantial, limited, or minimal improvement, while also acknowledging that the reasons for variation are not fully understood. Such figures depend on the patient group, the definition of improvement, the rehabilitation provided, and the time at which outcomes are measured. They should not be used to tell a patient that recovery is guaranteed or impossible. Prognosis should be based on individualized clinical assessment and updated as the patient's condition and response to rehabilitation become clearer.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a population-level recovery statistic as a cautious statement that preserves uncertainty for an individual patient."
Key point:
- Group statistics describe populations rather than determine individual outcomes.
- Prognosis requires individualized and repeated clinical assessment.
Source links:
- National Clinical Guideline for Stroke: Motor Recovery and Rehabilitation
48. Q: How can clinicians and patients distinguish useful compensatory plasticity from maladaptive change?
A: Show answer
The distinction requires examining whether a neural change improves meaningful function, causes unwanted symptoms, or produces both effects. A new pathway may help a patient complete a task but lead to inefficient movement patterns or excessive muscle activation. Cortical remapping after amputation may accompany phantom sensations or pain, while post-stroke recruitment of alternative motor areas may support movement. Clinicians should therefore combine functional testing, symptom reports, imaging or stimulation measures when appropriate, and observation of daily activities. A neural change should not be labeled beneficial solely because it is measurable; its impact on the person's goals, safety, comfort, and independence is what matters.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate one example of remapping using four criteria: functional gain, unwanted symptoms, movement quality, and transfer to daily life."
Key point:
- The value of plasticity depends on its functional and clinical consequences.
- Assessment should include both improvements and possible adverse effects.

49. Q: What practical lessons does the chapter offer for designing and evaluating new stroke-rehabilitation interventions?
A: Show answer
New interventions should be based on a clear hypothesis about the neural pathways they are intended to influence and should be evaluated using both neural and functional outcomes. Researchers need to consider lesion location, severity, stage of recovery, and the changing balance of activity between hemispheres. Stimulation protocols should be individualized and tested alongside meaningful rehabilitation rather than assumed to work independently. Trials should measure whether improvements transfer to daily activities and should monitor adverse effects. The chapter's discussion of TMS, tDCS, robotic rehabilitation, and fluoxetine shows that promising mechanisms require careful clinical testing before becoming standard practice.
Study instruction:
- "Draft an evaluation plan for one rehabilitation intervention, including target population, mechanism, comparison group, functional outcome, follow-up, and safety monitoring."
Key point:
- Rehabilitation innovation requires both mechanistic rationale and clinical evidence.
- Functional benefit and safety must be demonstrated, not assumed from neural effects.

- A Translational Roadmap for Brain Stimulation in Stroke Rehabilitation
- National Clinical Guideline for Stroke: Motor Recovery and Rehabilitation
50. Q: What are the most important lessons from the chapter about injury-induced neuroplasticity?
A: Show answer
The chapter shows that injury can reorganize neural pathways at several levels, from peripheral nerve fibers and spinal circuits to cortical maps and large-scale networks. After peripheral nerve injury or amputation, remapping may contribute to abnormal sensations and pain without restoring the lost function. After stroke, surviving pathways may be recruited and strengthened to support partial recovery, but these routes are often less efficient than the original pathways. Rehabilitation helps patients learn to use the neural resources that remain, while stimulation and other interventions are being studied as possible adjuncts. The effects of plasticity depend on timing, lesion characteristics, individual biology, and the quality of subsequent practice. The central lesson is that neural reorganization must be judged by what it enables the person to do, not merely by the fact that the brain has changed.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a chapter summary that links peripheral remapping, stroke compensation, rehabilitation, neuromodulation, and the need for evidence-based clinical evaluation."
Key point:
- Injury-induced plasticity can produce both maladaptive and compensatory changes.
- Recovery depends on surviving pathways, appropriate practice, timing, and individualized care.
