Table 8
Maladaptive Neuroplasticity in Addiction and Chronic Pain
1. Q: What is maladaptive neuroplasticity, and why are addiction and persistent pain important examples?
A: Show answer
Maladaptive neuroplasticity is a lasting change in neural structure or function that contributes to harmful behavior, symptoms, or reduced well-being rather than supporting useful adaptation. The chapter identifies addiction and persistent pain as two well-studied examples. Addictive substances alter reward and motivation circuits in ways that can promote craving and compulsive drug-seeking after the substance has left the body. Persistent pain can reorganize peripheral and spinal sensory pathways so that pain responses remain amplified after the original injury or stimulus has resolved. These examples show that plasticity is not inherently beneficial. The consequences depend on which circuits change, how they change, and whether the resulting activity supports or disrupts normal function.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish adaptive plasticity from maladaptive plasticity and explain how addiction and persistent pain illustrate the latter.
Key point:
- Neuroplasticity can produce harmful as well as beneficial adaptations.
- Addiction and chronic pain involve persistent changes in neural circuits.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
2. Q: How does the chapter describe the two-way relationship between brain plasticity and behavior?
A: Show answer
The chapter emphasizes that brain and behavior influence one another. Experiences and actions can induce plastic changes in neural circuits, and those changes can in turn influence future experiences, motivations, and behavior. Repeated drug use, for example, can alter reward pathways and strengthen associations between substances and environmental cues. Those altered circuits can then increase the likelihood of craving and further drug use. Persistent pain can also change the way sensory signals are processed, which may influence activity, avoidance, and future pain experience. This reciprocal relationship means that behavior is both a cause and a consequence of plasticity. It also explains why repeated patterns can become self-reinforcing.
Study instruction:
- Trace one feedback loop in addiction and one in persistent pain, identifying how an initial experience changes later behavior.
Key point:
- Experience changes neural circuits, and altered circuits influence later behavior.
- Repeated feedback can reinforce maladaptive patterns.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
3. Q: What is the mesolimbic pathway, and why is it central to the chapter's explanation of addiction?
A: Show answer
The mesolimbic pathway is a network involved in reward, motivation, and goal-directed behavior. The chapter describes it as beginning in the ventral tegmental area (VTA), a region of the midbrain containing dopamine-producing neurons that project to the nucleus accumbens. The nucleus accumbens then communicates with other areas, including cortical regions involved in memory and decision-making and the amygdala, which contributes to emotional processing. Addictive drugs act on this system and can increase dopamine transmission. Because the pathway normally helps translate motivation into actions that obtain natural rewards, changes within it can influence how strongly a person values and pursues a substance or other rewarding activity.
Study instruction:
- Draw the pathway from the VTA to the nucleus accumbens and then to cortical and limbic regions, noting the broad function of each area.
Key point:
- The mesolimbic pathway links dopamine signaling with reward and motivation.
- Addiction involves changes in a network rather than a single isolated brain centre.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
4. Q: What role does the ventral tegmental area play in reward-related behavior?
A: Show answer
The ventral tegmental area is a midbrain region containing dopamine-producing neurons that project to the nucleus accumbens and other areas. The chapter describes these neurons as an important source of dopamine signaling in the mesolimbic pathway. When natural rewards or addictive substances activate relevant circuits, VTA neurons can increase dopamine transmission. This signaling contributes to the processes through which the brain evaluates rewarding experiences and motivates goal-directed action. The VTA is not the only structure involved in reward, and dopamine is not its only biological function. Its importance lies in its position within a wider network that links reward signals to learning, motivation, and behavior.
Study instruction:
- Explain the VTA's position in the mesolimbic pathway and distinguish its role from that of the nucleus accumbens.
Key point:
- Dopamine-producing VTA neurons provide important input to the nucleus accumbens.
- Reward and motivation depend on coordinated activity across several brain regions.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
5. Q: What is the function of the nucleus accumbens in reward and motivation?
A: Show answer
The nucleus accumbens receives dopamine projections from the VTA and is part of the basal ganglia, a group of structures involved in learning, habit formation, and voluntary movement. The chapter describes it as playing a central role in evaluating rewarding experiences and translating motivation into goal-directed behavior. It also projects to cortical regions involved in memory and decision-making and to the amygdala, which contributes to emotional processing. The nucleus accumbens is often called the brain's reward centre, but that label is a simplification. Its function depends on its connections with a broader network and cannot be reduced to producing pleasure alone.
Study instruction:
- Identify the nucleus accumbens' main inputs and outputs and explain why the term reward centre is an incomplete description.
Key point:
- The nucleus accumbens integrates reward-related signals within a wider network.
- It contributes to learning, motivation, and action selection, not pleasure alone.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
6. Q: How does the amygdala contribute to the reward and motivation network described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The amygdala is involved in emotional processing, including fear, anxiety, and assigning emotional significance to experiences. The chapter describes it as one of the regions receiving projections from the nucleus accumbens. Its connections help link reward-related information with emotional and contextual learning. This is relevant to addiction because experiences, places, people, and objects associated with drug use can acquire strong motivational significance. The amygdala is not a stand-alone addiction centre; it participates in a network that includes the VTA, nucleus accumbens, cortex, and other structures. Understanding these connections helps explain why environmental cues can evoke strong emotional and behavioral responses.
Study instruction:
- Explain how emotional learning in the amygdala can contribute to the significance of cues associated with rewarding experiences.
Key point:
- The amygdala contributes emotional meaning to experiences.
- Reward-related behavior involves interaction between motivational and emotional circuits.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
7. Q: Why are the terms “reward centre” and “pleasure molecule” oversimplifications?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that the nucleus accumbens is popularly called the brain's reward centre and dopamine the pleasure molecule, but both descriptions are incomplete. The nucleus accumbens participates in learning, habit formation, motivation, and action selection through connections with multiple brain regions. Dopamine also serves functions beyond the experience of pleasure, including signaling that contributes to motivation and learning. A rewarding experience may involve dopamine release, but that does not mean dopamine is simply a chemical measure of how much pleasure a person feels. Using these simplified labels without qualification can obscure the network processes involved in addiction and lead to inaccurate explanations of behavior.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite the two popular labels as more accurate functional descriptions of the nucleus accumbens and dopamine.
Key point:
- Reward and pleasure are not controlled by one isolated centre or molecule.
- Dopamine participates in several functions, including motivation and learning.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
8. Q: How does the mesolimbic pathway normally help people pursue natural rewards?
A: Show answer
The mesolimbic pathway helps translate motivation into goal-directed actions that obtain natural rewards such as food, water, and sexual activity. The chapter describes rewarding experiences as activating VTA neurons and increasing dopamine release in the nucleus accumbens. The network then evaluates the significance of the experience and interacts with regions involved in memory, decision-making, and emotion. This arrangement supports learning about useful outcomes and motivating actions that help meet biological needs. Natural reward signaling is not inherently pathological. The problem arises when substances or repeated behaviors alter the system in ways that disproportionately strengthen reward-seeking and weaken control over behavior.
Study instruction:
- Trace how a natural reward can activate the mesolimbic pathway and influence a subsequent goal-directed action.
Key point:
- The mesolimbic pathway normally supports motivation toward natural rewards.
- Addiction involves changes to a system that also serves ordinary adaptive functions.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
9. Q: How do addictive drugs alter dopamine transmission in the mesolimbic pathway?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that addictive drugs act on the VTA and increase dopamine transmission through different mechanisms. Some substances increase the firing of dopamine-producing neurons directly or indirectly, while psychostimulants interfere with the removal of dopamine from the synaptic space. The resulting increase in dopamine can occur in the VTA, nucleus accumbens, and other projection areas. Although the drugs differ in their molecular actions, they converge on reward-related signaling. Repeated exposure can produce lasting changes in synaptic strength and neuronal structure, helping explain why drug-related motivation can persist after the substance has been cleared from the body.
Study instruction:
- Compare the broad mechanisms used by nicotine, opioids, cannabinoids, benzodiazepines, cocaine, and amphetamines to increase dopamine transmission.
Key point:
- Different drugs can converge on increased mesolimbic dopamine signaling.
- Repeated drug exposure can produce persistent changes in reward circuits.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
10. Q: How does nicotine increase dopamine-neuron activity in the ventral tegmental area?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that nicotine acts on nicotinic receptors expressed on the surface of dopamine-producing neurons in the VTA. This action increases their firing rate and thereby enhances dopamine transmission in the mesolimbic pathway. The mechanism is different from the indirect pathway described for opioids, cannabinoids, and benzodiazepines, which act through GABA-producing interneurons. Understanding the distinction is important because addictive substances do not all produce their effects through the same immediate molecular target. They can nevertheless converge on reward-related dopamine signaling and contribute to similar patterns of reinforcement and repeated use.
Study instruction:
- State the receptor type involved in nicotine's action and distinguish its direct effect on dopamine neurons from indirect disinhibition mechanisms.
Key point:
- Nicotine acts on nicotinic receptors in the VTA.
- Increased dopamine-neuron firing enhances mesolimbic signaling.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
11. Q: How do opioids, cannabinoids, and benzodiazepines increase dopamine-neuron firing according to the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes these substances as acting indirectly by inhibiting GABA-producing interneurons in the VTA. GABA normally provides inhibitory input that helps regulate the activity of dopamine-producing neurons. When the inhibitory interneurons are suppressed, the dopamine neurons are released from some of that inhibition and can fire more readily. This process is often described as disinhibition. It differs from nicotine's direct action on nicotinic receptors on dopamine neurons, even though both routes can increase dopamine signaling. The explanation is a simplified account of the mechanisms discussed in the chapter; each drug class has additional effects elsewhere in the nervous system.
Study instruction:
- Draw the inhibitory connection from GABA interneurons to dopamine neurons and show how reducing inhibition can increase firing.
Key point:
- These substances can increase dopamine activity through disinhibition in the VTA.
- Different molecular routes can produce a similar downstream increase in reward signaling.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
12. Q: How do cocaine and amphetamines affect dopamine transporters and synaptic dopamine levels?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes psychostimulants such as cocaine and amphetamines as blocking the dopamine transporter, a membrane protein that normally removes dopamine from the synaptic cleft after it has been released. When transporter activity is blocked, dopamine remains available in the synaptic space for longer, increasing dopamine signaling. This mechanism differs from the increased firing of dopamine neurons described for nicotine or the disinhibition mechanism described for some other drugs. The chapter groups several psychostimulants together for this broad explanation, although their detailed pharmacology is not identical. The important point is that interference with dopamine clearance can amplify signaling in reward-related pathways.
Study instruction:
- Explain the normal role of the dopamine transporter and predict what happens to synaptic dopamine when reuptake is blocked.
Key point:
- Dopamine transporters normally remove released dopamine from the synaptic cleft.
- Blocking transporter activity increases the duration or level of dopamine signaling.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
13. Q: Why does the chapter describe addictive drugs as hijacking the brain's reward pathway?
A: Show answer
Addictive drugs can enhance dopamine transmission in the mesolimbic pathway more strongly or in different patterns than ordinary natural rewards. This can produce powerful reinforcement and alter the relative value assigned to drug-related outcomes. Repeated exposure can strengthen associations between drug use and particular environments, people, or objects, while changes in reward and executive circuits can make drug-seeking increasingly habitual. The term hijacking describes how a system that normally supports adaptive motivation becomes strongly driven by a substance. It does not mean that the brain's reward system is permanently taken over in every person or that all users follow an identical progression.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a normally adaptive reward system can become biased toward a substance through repeated reinforcement and learning.
Key point:
- Addictive substances can disproportionately strengthen reward-related signaling.
- Repeated use can change the value and control of drug-seeking behavior.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
14. Q: What is long-term potentiation, and how is it involved in the early effects of addictive drugs?
A: Show answer
Long-term potentiation (LTP) is a persistent strengthening of synaptic transmission following particular patterns of activity. The chapter reports that a single dose of several addictive substances, including cocaine, morphine, nicotine, alcohol, and benzodiazepines, can induce LTP in the VTA. The reported effect can persist for up to about a week in the experimental work discussed. This provides a mechanism through which an initial drug exposure can produce synaptic changes that outlast the immediate chemical presence of the substance. The finding does not mean that one exposure inevitably causes addiction; it shows that drug exposure can alter synaptic strength in reward-related circuits.
Study instruction:
- Define LTP and explain why a synaptic change that lasts beyond the drug's immediate presence is relevant to learning and reinforcement.
Key point:
- LTP strengthens synaptic transmission for an extended period.
- Drug-induced LTP can alter reward circuitry beyond the immediate exposure.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
15. Q: What structural changes in dopamine-related neurons have been associated with exposure to cocaine and morphine?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes changes in dendritic-spine density in the VTA following exposure to addictive substances. Administration of cocaine or a related stimulant was associated with an increase in dendritic-spine density, whereas chronic morphine administration was associated with a decrease. These findings show that different substances can produce distinct structural effects even within the same broad reward-related region. Dendritic spines are sites of many excitatory synaptic contacts, so changes in their density can alter the potential organization of neural connections. The chapter notes that much of this evidence comes from experimental studies using mouse midbrain tissue, so the findings should not be treated as a complete description of human addiction.
Study instruction:
- Compare the reported direction of spine-density change after stimulant exposure and chronic morphine exposure, and identify the experimental limitation.
Key point:
- Different addictive substances can produce different structural changes.
- Dendritic-spine findings from animal models require cautious translation to humans.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
16. Q: What evidence links drug-induced dopamine changes in animal models with human experiences of reward?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that many mechanistic findings about LTP and dendritic-spine changes come from experiments using mouse midbrain tissue. Brain-imaging studies in humans provide a complementary line of evidence: addictive drugs increase dopamine concentration in the nucleus accumbens, and the size of that increase is closely associated with the pleasurable effects reported for the substances. The animal studies help investigate cellular mechanisms under controlled conditions, while human imaging shows that relevant dopamine changes also occur in people. Neither type of evidence alone explains the full development of addiction. Strong conclusions require combining experimental, imaging, behavioral, and clinical evidence while respecting the limits of each method.
Study instruction:
- Compare what animal tissue experiments can reveal about cellular mechanisms with what human brain imaging can reveal about dopamine changes during drug exposure.
Key point:
- Animal experiments and human imaging provide complementary evidence.
- No single method explains every stage or consequence of addiction.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
17. Q: How does the chapter describe the transition from recreational drug use to habitual or compulsive use?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a progression from voluntary use for pleasurable effects toward habitual use in which control over intake is reduced and the person feels compelled to seek the drug despite adverse consequences. Repeated exposure produces changes in reward-related circuits and in regions involved in memory and executive function. Drug use becomes associated with particular contexts and cues, and each subsequent dose can reinforce the behaviors that lead to obtaining the substance. The transition is not necessarily identical for every individual, and the chapter does not present a single event that marks the change. It emphasizes cumulative functional and structural adaptations that can make drug-seeking increasingly automatic and difficult to control.
Study instruction:
- Describe the behavioral and neural changes that distinguish voluntary recreational use from habitual or compulsive drug-seeking.
Key point:
- Addiction involves a shift toward reduced control and compulsive seeking.
- Repeated reinforcement and circuit changes contribute to the transition.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
18. Q: What is the binge-withdrawal-craving cycle described in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a cycle in which a person takes a drug to obtain its rewarding effects, then experiences withdrawal symptoms as the effects diminish. Withdrawal can trigger craving, which motivates the person to seek and take more of the substance. Repeated episodes can reinforce the association between discomfort, craving, and drug-taking behavior. The cycle helps explain why continued use may occur even when the person experiences serious adverse consequences. It is not simply a matter of seeking pleasure; avoiding or relieving withdrawal-related distress can become a powerful driver of repeated use. The cycle also illustrates how changes in reward and motivational circuits can maintain behavior over time.
Study instruction:
- Draw the cycle from drug use to withdrawal, craving, renewed seeking, and further use, and identify where environmental cues can strengthen it.
Key point:
- Addiction can be maintained by both reward seeking and avoidance of withdrawal.
- Craving can reinforce repeated drug-taking behavior.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
19. Q: How do environmental cues become associated with drug use and contribute to craving?
A: Show answer
Continued drug use can strengthen associations between the substance and the environments, people, objects, or routines present during use. The chapter explains that memory-related and executive regions become involved as repeated exposure teaches the user to associate drug-taking with particular contexts and cues. Later exposure to those cues can activate learned expectations and motivational responses, even when the substance itself is no longer present. This helps explain why craving can persist after the drug has been cleared from the brain. Cue-related learning is therefore an important part of the transition from initial reward to habitual and compulsive behavior.
Study instruction:
- Identify three possible cues associated with drug use and explain how repeated pairing can give them motivational significance.
Key point:
- Drug-related cues can acquire learned motivational value.
- Craving can be triggered by reminders of use, not only by the drug itself.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
20. Q: How do changes in memory and executive-function circuits contribute to compulsive drug use?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that continued drug use affects not only the initial reward pathway but also regions involved in memory and executive function. Memory systems help associate drug use with contexts and cues, while executive systems contribute to decision-making and behavioral control. As these networks adapt, the drug can become overvalued relative to other goals, and the behaviors leading to drug use can become habitual. This helps explain why a person may continue seeking a substance despite recognizing its adverse effects. The account does not imply that decision-making disappears entirely; rather, the balance between reward-driven motivation, learned associations, and control can shift in ways that make stopping difficult.
Study instruction:
- Explain how reward, memory, and executive function interact in the transition toward habitual drug-seeking.
Key point:
- Addiction involves a network beyond the initial reward response.
- Learned cues and altered control processes can reinforce compulsive behavior.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
21. Q: Can behaviors such as gambling, shopping, or sexual activity produce addiction-like patterns?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that activities such as gambling, sex, and shopping can engage reward-related systems and, in some circumstances, become compulsive. It describes evidence that these activities can lead to brain changes resembling aspects of those associated with substance-related addiction. The important distinction is between ordinary pleasurable behavior and a persistent pattern marked by loss of control, repeated engagement despite harm, and significant impairment. Not every frequent or enjoyable activity is an addiction. The chapter's discussion supports the idea that maladaptive reinforcement is not limited to substances, while careful assessment is needed before applying a clinical label to a particular behavior.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish frequent participation in a rewarding activity from a pattern involving impaired control and continuing harm.
Key point:
- Some non-substance behaviors can become compulsive and harmful.
- Enjoyment or frequency alone is not enough to establish addiction.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
22. Q: How can medication used to treat Parkinson's disease contribute to compulsive behaviors in some patients?
A: Show answer
Parkinson's disease involves degeneration of dopamine-producing cells in the substantia nigra, a midbrain region important for movement. The chapter explains that medications that increase dopamine signaling can alleviate some movement and cognitive symptoms. However, these medications can also act on the mesolimbic pathway. In rare cases, this may contribute to pathological gambling, hypersexuality, or other compulsive behaviors. The example demonstrates that a drug intended to improve one neural system can affect another system because dopamine pathways have multiple functions. It does not mean that such effects occur in every patient or that patients should change medication without medical advice.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between the substantia nigra's role in Parkinson's symptoms and the mesolimbic pathway's role in reward-related behavior.
Key point:
- Dopamine-enhancing medication can affect more than one neural pathway.
- Compulsive behaviors are reported in some cases and require clinical assessment.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
23. Q: Why is it important to distinguish the substantia nigra from the ventral tegmental area when discussing dopamine?
A: Show answer
Both are midbrain regions containing dopamine-producing neurons, but they contribute to different circuits and functions. The chapter describes degeneration of dopamine-producing cells in the substantia nigra as a central feature of Parkinson's disease and links this degeneration to movement deficits. The VTA, by contrast, is presented as the starting point of the mesolimbic reward pathway, projecting to the nucleus accumbens and other regions involved in motivation and reward. Dopamine-enhancing medications used for Parkinson's disease can affect both motor and reward-related systems. Treating all dopamine neurons as one interchangeable population would obscure these distinct pathways and their different consequences.
Study instruction:
- Compare the substantia nigra and VTA by location, major projections, and the functions emphasized in the chapter.
Key point:
- Dopamine neurons belong to distinct circuits with different roles.
- Medication effects depend partly on which pathways are influenced.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
24. Q: What is the normal protective function of physical pain?
A: Show answer
Physical pain alerts an organism to potentially damaging or life-threatening conditions and motivates actions that reduce further injury. The chapter describes pain as an evolutionarily important warning system. Pain can cause a person to withdraw from a harmful stimulus, protect an injured area, or seek assistance. The same system can become maladaptive when sensitization and neural reorganization persist after the original injury or stimulus has resolved. The distinction is therefore between acute pain that serves a protective function and persistent pathological pain that continues to disrupt function. Pain is not merely an unpleasant sensation; it is a signal processed by peripheral and central neural pathways that influences behavior.
Study instruction:
- Give one example of protective acute pain and one example of persistent pain that no longer serves the same immediate protective purpose.
Key point:
- Acute pain warns of potential tissue damage and promotes protection.
- Persistent pain can outlast its original protective role.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
25. Q: What are nociceptors, and where are their cell bodies located?
A: Show answer
Nociceptors are primary sensory neurons that detect potentially damaging mechanical, thermal, or chemical stimuli. The chapter explains that their cell bodies are clustered in the dorsal root ganglia, located just outside the spinal cord. Each neuron has a fiber that divides into two branches near the cell body. One branch extends toward the skin or another peripheral tissue, where receptors detect noxious stimuli. The other branch enters the spinal cord and forms synapses with second-order sensory neurons. This arrangement allows information about potential tissue damage to travel from the periphery into the central nervous system for further processing.
Study instruction:
- Draw a nociceptor with its cell body in the dorsal root ganglion and label the peripheral and central branches.
Key point:
- Nociceptors detect potentially damaging stimuli.
- Their cell bodies are located in dorsal root ganglia near the spinal cord.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
26. Q: What types of harmful stimuli can activate pain-sensing neurons?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes receptors on peripheral branches of nociceptors that respond to excessive mechanical pressure, harmful hot or cold temperatures, and certain chemicals released by damaged cells. These different inputs provide information about potentially damaging conditions. The receptors convert the stimulus into neural activity that travels toward the spinal cord. The chapter's description is a broad overview; individual nociceptors may respond to particular combinations of stimuli, and the mechanisms are more varied than a single receptor for each category. The key principle is that pain-sensing neurons detect conditions associated with actual or potential tissue damage and initiate signaling that can lead to protective behavior.
Study instruction:
- Classify three examples of harmful stimuli as mechanical, thermal, or chemical and explain how they can activate nociceptors.
Key point:
- Nociceptors respond to potentially damaging mechanical, thermal, and chemical stimuli.
- Peripheral detection begins the neural process that can produce pain perception.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
27. Q: How do pain signals travel from peripheral nociceptors to the brain?
A: Show answer
When nociceptors are activated, they generate nerve impulses that travel along their peripheral neurons toward the spinal cord. The central branches enter the spinal cord and form synapses with second-order sensory neurons. These second-order neurons transmit signals upward toward the brain, including the somatosensory cortex. Pain becomes consciously experienced after the signals have been processed by the relevant neural systems. This pathway shows that detecting a harmful stimulus and consciously perceiving pain are related but distinct stages. The signal must be transmitted and processed by central networks before it becomes an experience that can guide behavior.
Study instruction:
- Trace the signal from a peripheral nociceptor through the dorsal root ganglion and spinal cord to the somatosensory cortex.
Key point:
- Primary sensory neurons carry nociceptive information into the spinal cord.
- Second-order neurons relay information toward the brain for processing.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
28. Q: Why is the activation of a nociceptor not identical to the conscious experience of pain?
A: Show answer
Nociceptor activation is the detection and transmission of information about potentially harmful stimuli. Conscious pain requires further processing within the spinal cord and brain. The chapter explains that impulses from peripheral pain-sensing neurons are relayed to second-order neurons and then transmitted toward the somatosensory cortex. Only after the signals have been processed do we become aware of pain and respond to it. This distinction is important because the intensity of peripheral input does not by itself explain the full subjective experience. Central processing and plastic changes in the spinal cord and brain can alter how incoming signals are interpreted and experienced.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish nociception, the neural processing of harmful stimuli, from the conscious experience of pain.
Key point:
- Nociceptor activity provides input; pain perception requires central processing.
- Spinal and brain circuits can modify the experience generated by incoming signals.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
29. Q: What is peripheral sensitization, and how does it change the response of pain-sensing neurons?
A: Show answer
Peripheral sensitization occurs when the receptors and signaling machinery at the peripheral endings of nociceptors become more responsive after tissue injury or inflammation. The chapter explains that activation of protein sensors can cause them to redistribute within the nerve terminal and change their functional properties, lowering the threshold needed to activate the neuron. As a result, damaged tissue becomes hypersensitive and stimuli that would normally be harmless may be perceived as painful. Sensitization can also increase the firing rate of nociceptors and the probability of neurotransmitter release at their spinal terminals. These changes help protect an injured area but can contribute to persistent pain if they become prolonged or excessive.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a lower activation threshold changes the response to stimulation of injured tissue.
Key point:
- Peripheral sensitization increases the responsiveness of pain-sensing neurons.
- Lower thresholds can make normally harmless contact painful.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
30. Q: Why can sensitization after an injury be useful in the short term?
A: Show answer
Short-term sensitization can protect damaged tissue by making the affected area more sensitive to contact or movement. The chapter explains that this heightened sensitivity can encourage a person to avoid activities that might worsen the injury and thereby create conditions for repair. These changes are usually reversible when the underlying stimulus or inflammation resolves. The protective function becomes problematic when the sensitized state persists beyond the period in which it is useful. The distinction is therefore not that sensitization is always harmful, but that its duration and context matter. A temporary increase in sensitivity can be adaptive, while a lasting increase can contribute to chronic pain.
Study instruction:
- Compare the protective value of temporary sensitization with the consequences of prolonged sensitization after the original injury has healed.
Key point:
- Temporary hypersensitivity can limit contact with damaged tissue.
- Persistent sensitization can become maladaptive and contribute to chronic pain.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
31. Q: How can inflammation produce longer-lasting changes in peripheral pain pathways?
A: Show answer
During inflammation, damaged cells release growth factors and other signaling molecules that can influence nociceptors. The chapter explains that these signals can trigger the synthesis and trafficking of pain receptors and related signaling molecules within pain-sensing neurons. This increases the sensitivity of the cells to harmful stimuli and can sustain heightened responsiveness. The mechanism differs from the rapid redistribution of existing protein sensors because it can involve the production and movement of additional receptor and signaling components. These changes may outlast the immediate inflammatory stimulus and contribute to prolonged sensitivity. The chapter presents this as one route through which tissue inflammation can alter neural processing of pain.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish rapid changes in existing receptors from inflammation-related synthesis and trafficking of additional receptors and signaling molecules.
Key point:
- Inflammatory signals can alter the number and location of pain-related receptors.
- Peripheral changes can contribute to prolonged hypersensitivity.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
32. Q: What is central sensitization, and how does it differ from peripheral sensitization?
A: Show answer
Central sensitization refers to increased responsiveness within central pain-processing circuits, particularly at synapses in the spinal cord. Peripheral sensitization occurs at the endings of pain-sensing neurons in tissues, where receptor properties and activation thresholds change. The chapter explains that increased firing from sensitized peripheral neurons can increase neurotransmitter release at their spinal terminals. Repeated activity can then strengthen the response of second-order sensory neurons through synaptic plasticity. The distinction is important because persistent pain may involve changes at more than one level of the pathway. Treating all heightened pain sensitivity as a purely local tissue problem would overlook the contribution of spinal and brain circuits.
Study instruction:
- Compare the location and main mechanism of peripheral sensitization with the spinal synaptic changes involved in central sensitization.
Key point:
- Peripheral sensitization changes nociceptor responsiveness at the tissue end.
- Central sensitization changes the responsiveness of spinal or brain pain-processing circuits.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
33. Q: How can repeated activity in pain-sensing neurons strengthen synapses in the spinal cord?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that trains of impulses from sensitized nociceptors can induce long-term potentiation at synapses between primary sensory neurons and second-order sensory neurons in the spinal cord. LTP increases the strength of synaptic transmission, so subsequent incoming signals can produce a larger response in the second-order neurons. This can amplify pain signaling even when the original peripheral stimulus is relatively small. The mechanism illustrates how repeated activity can change the gain of a neural pathway. It also shows why persistent pain cannot always be explained by the intensity of the original injury alone; central synaptic changes can maintain heightened responsiveness.
Study instruction:
- Trace how increased nociceptor firing can lead to LTP at spinal synapses and amplified output from second-order neurons.
Key point:
- Repeated nociceptive input can strengthen spinal synaptic transmission.
- Central amplification can persist beyond the original peripheral stimulus.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
34. Q: What is wind-up in spinal pain processing?
A: Show answer
Wind-up is the progressively larger output of second-order sensory neurons when they receive repeated low-frequency input from pain-sensing neurons. The chapter describes it as a consequence of increased synaptic responsiveness following repeated trains of impulses. As the spinal circuit becomes more responsive, similar incoming signals can produce increasingly strong output. Wind-up is therefore a form of activity-dependent amplification within pain pathways. It helps explain how repeated nociceptive input can intensify pain processing even without a proportional increase in the original peripheral stimulus. It should be distinguished from the subjective report of pain intensity, because it describes a neural response mechanism rather than a complete measure of experience.
Study instruction:
- Describe how repeated low-frequency input can produce progressively larger output in second-order sensory neurons.
Key point:
- Wind-up is an activity-dependent increase in spinal neuronal output.
- Repeated input can amplify pain signaling without a matching increase in tissue damage.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
35. Q: How can persistent pain be associated with structural and functional changes in the primary somatosensory cortex?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that chronic or persistent pain is associated with changes in the primary somatosensory cortex, although the pattern differs according to the type of pain and injury. Cortical representations of affected body regions may expand or shrink, and these changes may influence how sensory information is processed. The chapter uses carpal tunnel syndrome and complex regional pain syndrome as contrasting examples. Such findings show that persistent pain can involve central reorganization rather than only ongoing activity at the injured tissue. However, an observed cortical change does not automatically prove that it caused the pain. The relationship may be reciprocal, with pain, altered use, and cortical plasticity influencing one another.
Study instruction:
- Explain why cortical representation changes should be considered in relation to the type of pain, body use, and the direction of causal evidence.
Key point:
- Persistent pain can be associated with cortical reorganization.
- The direction and meaning of cortical changes differ across pain conditions.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
36. Q: What cortical change has been reported in people with carpal tunnel syndrome?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that the cortical representation of the painful fingers can expand in people with carpal tunnel syndrome. It suggests that this expansion may contribute to the pain experienced by sufferers, although it does not establish that cortical enlargement is the only cause. The example illustrates that persistent pain can be associated with changes in the amount of cortical territory devoted to a body part. It also shows why pain research needs to consider both peripheral pathology and central processing. A cortical map is not simply a passive record of the body; it can change in response to altered input and may itself influence sensory experience.
Study instruction:
- Describe the reported direction of cortical change in carpal tunnel syndrome and distinguish the observation from the proposed explanation of pain.
Key point:
- The representation of painful fingers may expand in carpal tunnel syndrome.
- The contribution of this change to pain remains an interpretation rather than a complete causal account.

- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
37. Q: What cortical change has been described in complex regional pain syndrome?
A: Show answer
The chapter contrasts carpal tunnel syndrome with complex regional pain syndrome (CRPS), in which the cortical representation of the affected body part may shrink. It suggests that disuse could contribute to this reduction. The example demonstrates that chronic pain does not produce one universal pattern of cortical change. Depending on the condition, the affected body region, and patterns of use, a cortical representation may expand or contract. The chapter's wording is cautious: the proposed role of disuse is a possible explanation, not a proven mechanism in every patient. This contrast helps prevent overgeneralization from one pain condition to another.
Study instruction:
- Compare the cortical representation changes described for carpal tunnel syndrome and CRPS and identify the proposed role of disuse in the latter.
Key point:
- CRPS may be associated with a reduced cortical representation of the affected body part.

38. Q: Why should cortical map changes in chronic pain not be interpreted as identical across all conditions?
A: Show answer
The chapter explicitly notes that different kinds of pain and injuries affect the somatosensory cortex in different ways. Carpal tunnel syndrome is associated with expansion of the representation of painful fingers, while CRPS may involve shrinkage of the representation of the affected body part. These contrasting findings indicate that cortical reorganization depends on the underlying condition, the pattern of sensory input, and possibly changes in use or movement. A single rule such as “chronic pain always enlarges the affected representation” would therefore be inaccurate. Clinical and research interpretations need to identify the particular condition and evidence rather than assume one universal cortical response.
Study instruction:
- Use the two conditions in the chapter to challenge a generalized claim about how chronic pain changes cortical maps.
Key point:
- Chronic pain is associated with heterogeneous patterns of cortical reorganization.
- The condition and pattern of use matter when interpreting map changes.

- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
39. Q: What is the proposed sequence of cortical reorganization in persistent pain?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes cortical reorganization as occurring in stages. Within minutes of the initial injury, previously inhibited connections may become unmasked, allowing existing pathways to exert a stronger influence. Later, axonal sprouting may occur within the tissue being reorganized, creating additional structural connections. This sequence parallels the broader distinction between rapid functional changes and slower structural changes in other forms of neuroplasticity. The chapter does not claim that every patient follows an identical timetable or that these changes alone explain chronic pain. The proposed sequence is a framework for understanding how an initial injury can produce both immediate and longer-lasting changes in central sensory processing.
Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between early unmasking of existing connections and later axonal sprouting.
Key point:
- Early reorganization may involve rapid unmasking of existing connections.
- Later changes may include structural growth such as axonal sprouting.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
40. Q: How can unmasking of previously inhibited connections contribute to rapid changes in pain processing?
A: Show answer
Unmasking occurs when connections that were previously inhibited or functionally quiet become more influential after an injury or change in neural input. The chapter describes this as an early stage of cortical reorganization that can begin within minutes. Because the connections already exist, their influence can change more rapidly than would be possible through the growth of new axons. This can alter the way sensory information is represented and processed before longer-term structural changes occur. Unmasking is one proposed mechanism, not a complete explanation of all persistent pain. Its importance is that it helps account for rapid changes in neural responsiveness after injury.
Study instruction:
- Explain why unmasking can occur more quickly than axonal sprouting and how it may alter the influence of existing sensory connections.
Key point:
- Unmasking changes the functional influence of existing connections.
- Rapid functional reorganization can precede structural growth.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
41. Q: How can axonal sprouting contribute to longer-lasting changes in pain-related cortical networks?
A: Show answer
Axonal sprouting is the growth of new branches from existing nerve fibers. The chapter identifies it as a later stage of cortical reorganization after pain-related injury. New branches can create additional connections and alter the pathways through which sensory information is transmitted. Unlike rapid unmasking, sprouting involves structural change and may help consolidate a new pattern of neural connectivity. The chapter does not specify that sprouting is always harmful or that it necessarily produces chronic pain. Its effects depend on which fibers grow, where they connect, and how the resulting network processes sensory input. The mechanism illustrates how persistent pain can involve structural plasticity as well as altered activity.
Study instruction:
- Compare axonal sprouting with the early unmasking of existing connections and explain why the functional consequences depend on the new targets.
Key point:
- Axonal sprouting creates new structural connections.
- Its effects depend on the pathways and targets involved.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
42. Q: How are addiction and persistent pain similar as examples of maladaptive neuroplasticity?
A: Show answer
Both conditions involve neural changes that can persist beyond the immediate presence of the original trigger. In addiction, drug exposure alters reward and motivation circuits, strengthening learned associations and compulsive drug-seeking. In persistent pain, injury and inflammation can sensitize peripheral nociceptors, strengthen spinal synapses, and reorganize cortical representations. Both examples show that repeated activity can change the way neural systems respond in the future. However, the circuits and mechanisms differ: addiction primarily involves reward, motivation, memory, and executive networks, while persistent pain involves nociceptive pathways and sensory-processing circuits. The comparison is useful because it reveals a shared principle without treating the conditions as biologically identical.
Study instruction:
- Create a comparison of the trigger, principal circuits, plastic mechanisms, and behavioral consequences in addiction and persistent pain.
Key point:
- Both conditions involve lasting changes in neural processing.
- Similar principles of plasticity operate in different circuits and produce different symptoms.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
43. Q: What would explain why a person experiences craving when encountering a place associated with past drug use, even after the drug has left the body?
A: Show answer
Repeated drug use can strengthen associations between the substance and the environments, people, objects, or routines present during use. Memory and motivational circuits learn these relationships, while changes in the mesolimbic pathway can make drug-related cues highly salient. Later exposure to a familiar place can reactivate learned expectations and motivational responses, producing craving even when no drug is present. This illustrates why the persistence of addiction cannot be explained only by the time a substance remains in the bloodstream or brain. Learned neural adaptations can outlast the immediate pharmacological effect and influence future behavior.
Study instruction:
- Identify the cue, the learned association, and the reward-related circuit changes that could contribute to craving in this scenario.
Key point:
- Environmental cues can trigger learned craving after the substance has cleared.
- Persistent addiction-related plasticity involves memory and motivation as well as immediate drug effects.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
44. Q: What could explain why light contact with an injured area becomes painful during inflammation?
A: Show answer
Inflammation can release growth factors and other signals that alter the receptors and signaling molecules in peripheral pain-sensing neurons. These changes can lower the activation threshold, making nociceptors respond more strongly to stimulation. As a result, contact that would normally be harmless may be perceived as painful. The heightened sensitivity can initially protect the injured area by discouraging contact, but it may become maladaptive if it persists after the tissue has recovered. The scenario illustrates peripheral sensitization and shows how changes in receptor properties can alter the relationship between a physical stimulus and the neural response.
Study instruction:
- Trace the sequence from inflammation to receptor changes, lowered activation threshold, and increased sensitivity to touch.
Key point:
- Inflammatory signals can sensitize peripheral nociceptors.
- Lower thresholds can cause normally innocuous stimuli to be experienced as painful.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
45. Q: How would you explain progressively stronger spinal responses to repeated low-frequency pain signals?
A: Show answer
The pattern is consistent with wind-up, in which repeated low-frequency input from nociceptors produces progressively larger output from second-order sensory neurons in the spinal cord. Repeated activity can induce LTP at the relevant synapses, strengthening transmission and increasing the response to subsequent input. The result is a spinal circuit that amplifies incoming signals. This does not mean that the original tissue injury must be worsening each time the response increases. The neural pathway itself may have become more responsive through activity-dependent plasticity. The explanation should distinguish the intensity of the peripheral stimulus from the gain of the central pathway.
Study instruction:
- Explain how repeated input can increase spinal output even when the strength of the incoming stimulus remains similar.
Key point:
- Wind-up reflects increasing responsiveness in spinal pain circuits.
- Central amplification can change the relationship between input and perceived pain.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
46. Q: How might reduced use of a painful body part influence its cortical representation?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests that disuse may contribute to shrinkage of the cortical representation of an affected body part in complex regional pain syndrome. When a person avoids using a painful area, the pattern of sensory input and movement-related activity reaching the brain may change. Over time, this altered use may influence the organization of cortical maps. The chapter presents disuse as a possible contributor rather than a proven sole cause. The relationship may be reciprocal: pain can lead to reduced use, and altered use may in turn influence cortical representation and sensory processing. This is why persistent pain needs to be understood in relation to both neural mechanisms and behavior.
Study instruction:
- Explain the possible feedback loop between pain, reduced use, and cortical representation, while distinguishing a proposed mechanism from established causation.
Key point:
- Disuse may contribute to changes in cortical representation.
- Pain and behavior can influence one another over time.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
47. Q: What evidence would be needed to determine whether cortical reorganization causes persistent pain or results from it?
A: Show answer
A correlation between pain and altered cortical representation does not establish which process came first or whether one causes the other. Researchers would need longitudinal evidence showing how cortical maps and pain change over time, careful comparison with appropriate control groups, and methods that can test whether modifying the relevant neural activity changes pain outcomes. They would also need to account for injury severity, inflammation, movement, disuse, medication, and other factors that may influence both pain and cortical organization. The chapter's examples are useful evidence of association and plausible mechanisms, but they do not prove that cortical map changes are the sole cause of chronic pain.
Study instruction:
- Design a basic research question that separates correlation, temporal sequence, and causal intervention in the study of chronic pain.
Key point:
- Association does not establish whether cortical change causes pain or follows it.
- Longitudinal and intervention evidence can strengthen causal conclusions.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
48. Q: Why is a dopamine-centred explanation of addiction incomplete?
A: Show answer
Dopamine signaling is important to the mesolimbic reward pathway, but the chapter describes addiction as involving a wider network of structures and processes. The VTA and nucleus accumbens participate in reward-related signaling, while cortical regions contribute to memory and decision-making and the amygdala contributes emotional significance. Repeated drug use can produce synaptic and structural changes, strengthen cue associations, alter habits, and affect executive control. Withdrawal and craving also contribute to repeated use. A dopamine-centred explanation captures an important part of the process but cannot explain every aspect of compulsive behavior, individual differences, or recovery. Addiction is better understood as a network-level condition involving interacting biological and behavioral processes.
Study instruction:
- Identify at least three non-dopamine processes or brain regions that contribute to the chapter's explanation of addiction.
Key point:
- Dopamine is important but not sufficient to explain addiction.
- Memory, emotion, habit, and executive networks also contribute.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
49. Q: What practical lesson should be drawn from the chapter's account of addiction and persistent pain without overstating its clinical implications?
A: Show answer
The chapter shows that repeated drug exposure and persistent nociceptive activity can produce changes that outlast the original trigger. This supports careful assessment of learned cues, withdrawal, sensitization, and central amplification rather than assuming that current symptoms are explained only by the substance or injury present at the beginning. However, the chapter is primarily a mechanistic account and does not provide a complete treatment protocol. Clinical decisions about addiction or persistent pain require current evidence, individualized assessment, and qualified professional care. Mechanistic understanding can guide research and help explain symptoms, but it should not be converted into unsupported claims about a particular therapy or guaranteed outcome.
Study instruction:
- Separate the mechanisms explained in the chapter from treatment decisions that require current clinical guidance and individual assessment.
Key point:
- Neural mechanisms help explain persistent symptoms but do not by themselves prescribe treatment.
- Clinical management requires evidence-based, individualized assessment.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
50. Q: What are the most important lessons to remember from the chapter on addiction and pain?
A: Show answer
The chapter explains that addiction and persistent pain are examples of maladaptive neuroplasticity. Addictive substances alter dopamine transmission in the mesolimbic pathway and can induce lasting synaptic and structural changes. Repeated use strengthens associations with environmental cues, affects memory and executive networks, and can contribute to habitual or compulsive drug-seeking. Pain begins with nociceptors that detect potentially damaging stimuli and transmit signals through the spinal cord to the brain. Injury and inflammation can sensitize peripheral receptors, increase firing and transmitter release, induce LTP at spinal synapses, and produce wind-up. Persistent pain can also be associated with cortical reorganization, although patterns differ across conditions and causal relationships require careful study. The central lesson is that repeated experience can reshape neural circuits in ways that influence future behavior and perception, sometimes long after the original trigger has disappeared.
Study instruction:
- Review the chapter by explaining one reward-circuit mechanism, one pain-pathway mechanism, and one example of lasting maladaptive plasticity, including the limits of the evidence.
Key point:
- Addiction involves lasting changes in reward, motivation, memory, and control networks.
- Persistent pain can involve peripheral sensitization, spinal amplification, and cortical reorganization.
- Plasticity is context-dependent and can maintain harmful patterns as well as support adaptation.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
