Table 10
Neuroplasticity: Mechanisms, Limits, and Individual Differences
1. Q: What is the main conclusion of the book's final chapter about the nature of the brain?
A: Show answer
The chapter rejects the idea that the mature brain is a fixed, immutable structure. Neuroplasticity is a collective term for changes in the nervous system, from short-lived changes in neuronal electrical properties to long-term alterations in synapses, myelin, and large-scale connectivity. These changes occur during development and continue throughout adulthood, although their extent and mechanisms vary with age, region, experience, and individual biology. The chapter also cautions against treating plasticity as unlimited or magical. The brain can adapt, but it has physical constraints, and the same capacity for change can support learning, compensation, or maladaptive outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the shift from the fixed-brain view to the modern view of a dynamic nervous system, then identify one limit on that change."
Key point:
- The brain remains dynamic throughout life rather than becoming completely fixed after development.
- Plasticity creates opportunities for adaptation but is constrained and can have negative consequences.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
2. Q: Why did the idea of an immutable adult brain become outdated?
A: Show answer
Early neuroscience emphasized the stability of mature neural circuits, and Santiago Ramón y Cajal is often associated with the claim that the adult brain is fixed. The chapter notes that Cajal's position was more nuanced than the famous phrase suggests. Later research demonstrated that synaptic strength, connectivity, neuronal structure, and functional organization can change with learning, experience, injury, and other conditions. The important historical correction is not that the brain changes without limits, but that stability and change coexist. Mature circuits are more constrained than developing ones in many respects, yet they retain meaningful capacity for modification.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how later experimental evidence revised the fixed-brain model without replacing it with an equally simplistic claim of unlimited plasticity."
Key point:
- The fixed-brain view was revised by evidence of experience-dependent changes in mature neural systems.
- Adult plasticity is real but does not imply unlimited or effortless transformation.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
3. Q: What does the term neuroplasticity include?
A: Show answer
Neuroplasticity includes the processes that alter the structure or function of the nervous system. At the cellular level, synapses can strengthen, weaken, appear, or disappear. Neurons can change their electrical properties or the neurotransmitters they use. Oligodendrocytes can alter myelin formation, and networks can reorganize their functional connections. At larger scales, training or injury may be associated with changes in cortical representations or white-matter pathways. These processes operate over different timescales and can occur together. Neuroplasticity is therefore not one mechanism or a synonym for “rewiring”; it is an umbrella concept for many forms of nervous-system change.
Study instruction:
- "Create a concept map with synaptic, cellular, myelin-related, and network-level examples of neuroplasticity."
Key point:
- Neuroplasticity is an umbrella term covering multiple forms and levels of nervous-system change.
- The term should not be reduced to one process such as synaptic strengthening or new-neuron growth.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
4. Q: At what levels of organization can neuroplastic changes occur?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes plasticity across levels ranging from molecular and genetic processes to neuronal electrical properties, synaptic connections, cellular structure, local circuits, and large-scale brain networks. A change in receptor expression can alter how a synapse responds; repeated activity can change synaptic strength; oligodendrocytes can alter myelin around axons; and training can be associated with changes in broader connectivity. These levels are related but not identical. A molecular event does not automatically predict a behavioral outcome, because many intermediate processes and environmental influences shape the final effect. A complete explanation therefore identifies the level being studied and avoids assuming that a finding at one scale explains every other scale.
Study instruction:
- "Take one experience, such as learning a motor skill, and identify possible changes at molecular, synaptic, cellular, and network levels."
Key point:
- Plasticity can occur from molecular events through synapses and cells to distributed networks.
- Evidence at one organizational level does not automatically establish effects at all other levels.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
5. Q: How do the timescales of neuroplasticity differ?
A: Show answer
Neuroplastic changes can occur over very different timescales. Some changes in neuronal electrical properties last milliseconds or seconds, while synaptic modifications may persist for hours, days, or longer. Structural changes, such as axonal growth or altered myelination, may develop over weeks, months, or years. The chapter emphasizes that plasticity is not a single event with one characteristic speed. A rapid functional adjustment can precede slower structural remodeling, and several processes may interact during learning or recovery. Timescale matters because a brief change in activity should not be confused with a durable alteration in circuit structure or long-term behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Arrange examples of plasticity from fastest to slowest and explain why the time course changes the interpretation of a finding."
Key point:
- Plasticity ranges from rapid electrical changes to gradual structural remodeling.
- A short-lived functional response is not equivalent to a lasting structural change.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
6. Q: Why are synaptic strengthening and weakening important forms of plasticity?
A: Show answer
Synapses are communication points between neurons, and their effectiveness can change with patterns of activity. Strengthening or weakening a synapse alters how strongly one neuron influences another, which can help encode information, refine circuits, and support learning and memory. The chapter describes these changes as occurring continuously in the brain. They are not simply “more is better”: weakening can be as important as strengthening because circuits need to select, refine, and balance information. Synaptic change also interacts with structural processes such as the creation or elimination of synapses. The result is a flexible network whose connections are continually adjusted rather than permanently fixed.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why a useful learning system needs both increases and decreases in synaptic effectiveness."
Key point:
- Synaptic strengthening and weakening are central mechanisms for adjusting neural communication.
- Effective learning requires refinement and balance, not indiscriminate strengthening of every connection.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
7. Q: How can synapses be created, strengthened, weakened, or eliminated during experience?
A: Show answer
Experience can influence whether connections between neurons become more effective, less effective, newly formed, or removed. Repeated coordinated activity may strengthen some connections, while reduced or competing activity can weaken others. Development and learning can also involve the formation and elimination of synapses as circuits become more specialized. These processes are influenced by the type of experience, the brain region, developmental stage, and local network conditions. The chapter treats them as part of the continuous maintenance and adaptation of neural circuits. The important point is that learning does not consist only of adding connections; it also involves selective refinement and removal.
Study instruction:
- "Describe one example in which strengthening a useful pathway and weakening or removing competing connections could jointly improve circuit function."
Key point:
- Experience can change both the effectiveness and the number of synaptic connections.
- Plasticity includes selective refinement and elimination as well as growth.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
8. Q: Does neuroplasticity stop when the brain reaches adulthood?
A: Show answer
No. The chapter emphasizes that the adult brain continues to change in response to experience, learning, injury, and environmental demands. Adult plasticity may be more limited or slower for some processes than plasticity during early development, but synaptic changes, activity-dependent myelination, and certain forms of circuit reorganization remain possible. The extent of change depends on the mechanism and the neural system involved. It is therefore inaccurate to say either that adult brains are fixed or that adults can reorganize any function without constraint. The better conclusion is that plasticity is lifelong but varies across ages, regions, and types of experience.
Study instruction:
- "Give two examples of adult plasticity from the chapter and explain why they do not imply unlimited adult recovery."
Key point:
- Adult brains retain meaningful capacity for experience-dependent change.
- The type and extent of adult plasticity depend on the system and the conditions.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
9. Q: Why is plasticity generally greater during early development than in adulthood?
A: Show answer
During development, neural circuits are being established and refined, so experience can have a particularly strong influence on their organization. Sensory systems, for example, may pass through periods when input is especially important for shaping normal function. As circuits mature, structural and molecular constraints can stabilize their organization, making some changes harder to induce. The chapter describes a general decline in plastic capacity with age, but this should not be treated as a universal rule for every brain process. Some forms of plasticity remain active throughout life, and adult learning can still produce measurable changes. The key is that developmental timing changes the opportunities and constraints for adaptation.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a developing sensory circuit with an adult circuit and identify why the same experience may produce different effects at different ages."
Key point:
- Developing circuits are often more sensitive to experience because their organization is still being established.
- Age-related differences are mechanism-specific and do not mean that adult learning is impossible.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
10. Q: What is a critical period, and why does it matter for brain development?
A: Show answer
A critical period is a limited developmental window during which particular experiences have unusually strong or sometimes essential effects on the organization of a neural system. The chapter uses early visual development to illustrate how experience can shape cortical circuits. If normal input is absent or altered during a critical period, later experience may not fully reverse the resulting functional changes. Critical periods are not identical across all brain systems, and they should not be confused with the claim that learning ends after childhood. Research also distinguishes critical periods from broader sensitive periods in which experience has especially strong but not absolutely exclusive influence.
Study instruction:
- "Define a critical period and give one reason why the timing of sensory experience can matter for later function."
Key point:
- Critical periods are time windows when experience has unusually strong influence on selected neural circuits.
- Their timing and consequences vary across systems; they do not mean all learning stops afterward.
Source links:
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
11. Q: How does early sensory experience shape developing neural circuits?
A: Show answer
During development, sensory inputs help organize connections and refine how neural circuits respond to the environment. In the visual system, competing inputs from the two eyes influence the organization of the primary visual cortex during a critical period. When input is disrupted, cortical responses can shift in ways that affect later visual function. These processes involve both synaptic changes and broader network mechanisms, including the maturation of inhibitory circuits. The chapter's point is that early experience is not merely added to an already complete brain; it helps shape the circuits that will process future experience. The specific effects depend on the sensory system and developmental timing.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how competing sensory inputs can shape a developing cortical representation, using the visual system as the example."
Key point:
- Early sensory input helps organize and refine developing neural circuits.
- The effects depend on timing, circuit competition, and the maturation of local networks.
Source links:
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
12. Q: Why can early injury sometimes be followed by greater functional reorganization than comparable adult injury?
A: Show answer
Developing brains often have greater capacity for some forms of circuit reorganization, so neighboring or connected regions may be able to assume functions or support compensation more effectively after early injury. The chapter gives the example of a child who can sometimes lead a relatively typical life after removal of an entire cerebral hemisphere, whereas comparable surgery in adulthood would generally have very different consequences. This is an illustration of developmental plasticity, not a prediction for every child or a recommendation for surgery. Outcomes depend on the condition, timing, remaining networks, rehabilitation, and many individual factors. Greater plasticity can improve adaptation, but it does not guarantee complete recovery.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the hemisphere-removal example illustrates developmental plasticity while also identifying why it cannot be generalized to every child or injury."
Key point:
- Some developing brains can reorganize functions more extensively after injury than adult brains.
- Greater developmental plasticity improves possibilities for compensation but does not guarantee normal function.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
13. Q: What does the chapter mean when it says that plasticity tends to decrease with age?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses this as a broad tendency: many forms of learning and large-scale reorganization are easier or more pronounced during development than later in life. It does not mean that every form of plasticity declines at the same rate, that older adults cannot learn, or that the brain stops changing. Different systems have different developmental schedules, and adult plasticity can remain substantial for particular tasks and mechanisms. The appropriate interpretation is that age changes the conditions under which plasticity occurs. Claims about age should therefore specify the neural process, task, population, and outcome rather than treating the brain as uniformly more or less plastic at every age.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite the broad statement “plasticity decreases with age” as a qualified scientific claim that includes exceptions and mechanism-specific variation."
Key point:
- Age can alter the capacity and conditions for plastic change.
- A general age-related trend should not be mistaken for a universal loss of learning ability.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
14. Q: Why is neuroplasticity not a miracle cure or a source of unlimited personal transformation?
A: Show answer
The chapter warns that public discussions sometimes give neuroplasticity near-magical powers. In reality, neural change is constrained by existing anatomy, physiology, health, developmental history, and the type of intervention or experience. Plasticity can support learning and partial recovery, but it cannot guarantee that every lost function will return or that any desired skill can be acquired without limits. Some attempts to enhance plasticity remain experimental and may produce modest or uncertain benefits. Plasticity can also maintain harmful patterns, as shown by addiction and chronic pain. A responsible account recognizes both the genuine adaptability of the nervous system and the biological constraints that shape its outcomes.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a claim that a particular exercise or app can “rewire the brain” and identify the evidence needed to support such a claim."
Key point:
- Neuroplasticity creates real opportunities but is constrained by biological and practical limits.
- It is not a guaranteed cure, an unlimited resource, or proof that every brain-training claim is effective.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
15. Q: How can the same plastic mechanism produce different effects in different brain regions?
A: Show answer
The effect of plasticity depends on where it occurs, when it occurs, which cells and connections are involved, and the activity patterns that trigger it. A change that strengthens a useful sensory or motor pathway may improve performance, while a similar increase in a maladaptive circuit may reinforce an unwanted response. The chapter emphasizes that the same mechanisms can have different consequences depending on their location and timing. Conversely, a similar behavioral outcome may arise through different combinations of cellular and network changes. This context dependence is why neuroplasticity cannot be understood as a single switch that is always beneficial when turned on.
Study instruction:
- "Use two hypothetical circuits to show how a similar change in synaptic strength could have different behavioral consequences."
Key point:
- The consequences of plasticity depend on location, timing, cell type, and activity pattern.
- The same broad mechanism can support useful adaptation or contribute to dysfunction.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
16. Q: Why can several forms of neuroplasticity act together during one experience?
A: Show answer
A learning experience can alter synaptic strength, receptor expression, neuronal excitability, myelin formation, and the coordination of distributed circuits. These changes may occur at different times and scales but contribute to the same behavioral outcome. The chapter argues that different types of plasticity can operate separately or in concert, depending on the circumstances. For example, learning a motor skill may involve changes in synapses within motor networks and activity-dependent changes in myelination that influence communication along axons. Researchers are still working to determine how these levels relate to one another in living brains. It is therefore important not to assume that one observed mechanism fully explains the whole learning process.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a learned skill and list several forms of plasticity that might contribute at different organizational levels."
Key point:
- Complex experiences can recruit multiple forms of plasticity at once.
- A single observed change rarely explains the entire behavioral outcome.
Source links:
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
17. Q: What role does myelin play in neural communication?
A: Show answer
Myelin is a lipid-rich insulating material that wraps around segments of many axons. In the central nervous system it is produced by oligodendrocytes, while Schwann cells produce myelin in the peripheral nervous system. Myelin helps electrical impulses travel efficiently along axons by reducing current leakage and supporting rapid conduction between gaps known as nodes of Ranvier. The chapter emphasizes that myelin is not merely a static coating. Its formation and distribution can change, and those changes may influence the speed and coordination of signaling between neurons. Because neural timing matters for network function, myelin-related plasticity can be relevant to learning and adaptation.
Study instruction:
- "Draw a myelinated axon and label the myelin segments, nodes of Ranvier, oligodendrocytes, and the direction of impulse conduction."
Key point:
- Myelin supports efficient conduction along axons and is produced by different glial cells in the CNS and PNS.
- Myelin can be involved in plasticity rather than serving only as a fixed insulating layer.
Source links:
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
18. Q: How do oligodendrocytes and Schwann cells differ in myelin production?
A: Show answer
Oligodendrocytes produce myelin in the central nervous system, including the brain and spinal cord. A single oligodendrocyte can extend processes that form myelin sheaths around segments of multiple axons. Schwann cells produce myelin in the peripheral nervous system, generally wrapping one segment of one axon. The chapter uses this distinction to explain the organization of myelinated fibers and to introduce the possibility that myelin distribution can change with activity. The two cell types have related insulating functions but occupy different anatomical systems and organize their sheaths differently. This distinction is important when interpreting research on central myelination and when considering diseases that affect myelin.
Study instruction:
- "Compare oligodendrocytes and Schwann cells by location, number of axon segments supported, and role in neural conduction."
Key point:
- Oligodendrocytes myelinate CNS axons; Schwann cells myelinate PNS axons.
- Their related functions occur in different anatomical contexts and with different cellular arrangements.
Source links:
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
19. Q: What are nodes of Ranvier, and why are they important?
A: Show answer
Nodes of Ranvier are short gaps between adjacent myelin segments along a myelinated axon. They contain specialized membrane proteins that allow the electrical impulse to be regenerated as it travels along the fiber. Because the signal effectively moves from node to node, conduction can be much faster than it would be along an unmyelinated axon of similar diameter. This is often called saltatory conduction. The chapter introduces the nodes to explain why myelin distribution matters for the timing and efficiency of neural communication. Changes in myelin can therefore influence more than insulation; they can alter how reliably and synchronously information travels through a network.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why an impulse is regenerated at nodes rather than continuously along every part of a myelinated axon."
Key point:
- Nodes of Ranvier are gaps between myelin segments where action potentials are regenerated.
- The arrangement supports rapid saltatory conduction and helps coordinate neural signaling.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
20. Q: What evidence suggests that myelin can change in response to neuronal activity?
A: Show answer
The chapter summarizes animal research showing that neuronal activity can influence the formation and distribution of myelin. In one line of work, motor-skill learning in mice was associated with a temporary increase in the production of new oligodendrocytes, and preventing that new cell formation interfered with mastering the task. Other experiments found that blocking synaptic vesicle release reduced the number of myelin sheaths formed by individual oligodendrocytes, while stimulating activity increased sheath formation. These findings support activity-dependent myelination. They do not prove that every human learning task produces the same change, but they show that myelin is responsive to neural activity in experimental systems.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the evidence linking neuronal activity with oligodendrocyte formation or myelin sheath number, and identify the species and methods involved."
Key point:
- Experimental studies show that neuronal activity can influence myelin formation.
- Animal findings support a mechanism but should not be generalized directly to all human learning.
Source links:
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
21. Q: How does motor-skill learning provide evidence for activity-dependent myelination?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes experiments in which adult mice learned to run on a wheel with irregularly spaced rungs. During learning, production of new oligodendrocytes increased temporarily, and blocking the formation of these new cells prevented the animals from mastering the task. The findings suggest that new myelin can contribute to the acquisition of a motor skill rather than simply appearing as a passive consequence after learning is complete. The study was performed in animals and involved a specific task, so it does not establish that every human skill depends on the same degree of new myelination. It nevertheless demonstrates that glial changes can participate actively in experience-dependent adaptation.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the experimental logic of blocking new oligodendrocyte formation and why that helps test whether myelination contributes to learning."
Key point:
- Motor-skill learning in mice was linked to increased production of new oligodendrocytes.
- Interfering with new oligodendrocyte formation impaired task mastery in that experimental model.
Source links:
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
22. Q: What did research on synaptic vesicle release and oligodendrocytes reveal?
A: Show answer
The chapter cites research showing that synaptic vesicle release regulates the number of myelin sheaths formed by individual oligodendrocytes in vivo. When synaptic vesicle release was blocked, fewer sheaths were produced during the relevant formation period; stimulating neuronal activity increased sheath formation. This suggests that signals associated with neuronal activity can influence the myelinating behavior of oligodendrocytes. The study provides evidence for communication between active neural circuits and glial cells. It does not mean that every increase in activity creates more myelin in every region or that myelin changes always improve performance. The result is a specific experimental demonstration of activity-dependent regulation.
Study instruction:
- "State the manipulation, observed myelin effect, and main conclusion of the cited oligodendrocyte study."
Key point:
- Synaptic activity can regulate how many myelin sheaths an oligodendrocyte forms.
- The finding supports neuron-glia interaction as part of neural plasticity.
Source links:
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
23. Q: How might activity-dependent redistribution of myelin affect communication between distant brain regions?
A: Show answer
If myelin is added or redistributed on particular axons, the speed and timing of signals along those pathways may change. Coordinated activity across distant regions depends not only on whether signals arrive but also on when they arrive relative to one another. The chapter therefore suggests that activity-dependent myelin changes could influence synchrony between brain regions and, in turn, information processing. This is a plausible network-level consequence of changing conduction properties, not proof that every observed myelin change directly causes a measurable behavioral improvement. The broader lesson is that glial plasticity may contribute to the timing and coordination of neural communication as well as to conduction speed.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how changing conduction time along one pathway could alter the timing relationship between two connected brain regions."
Key point:
- Myelin distribution can influence conduction timing and potentially network synchrony.
- The proposed effect on large-scale information processing requires careful evidence and should not be overstated.
Source links:
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
24. Q: Why is it misleading to describe myelin as a completely stable structure?
A: Show answer
Myelin distribution is often relatively stable compared with rapid synaptic activity, but the chapter reviews evidence that myelin can change during development, training, and injury-related adaptation. New myelin can be added over weeks or months, and some animal studies suggest that activity-related changes can occur on shorter timescales. The chapter therefore challenges the assumption that myelin is simply a permanent insulating sheath laid down once and never adjusted. At the same time, it does not imply that myelin is constantly remodeled everywhere or that all observed changes are functionally important. Stability and plasticity coexist, and the balance depends on region, age, activity, and biological conditions.
Study instruction:
- "Contrast the relative stability of established myelin with experimental evidence for activity-dependent changes in new myelin formation."
Key point:
- Myelin is relatively stable in many contexts but can be remodeled.
- Evidence for myelin plasticity does not imply continuous or unlimited remodeling throughout the brain.
Source links:
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
25. Q: What is neurotransmitter switching, and why does it challenge older assumptions about neuronal identity?
A: Show answer
Neurons are often classified by the neurotransmitter they synthesize and release, such as dopamine, GABA, or glutamate. The chapter describes evidence that some mature neurons can alter the transmitter they use, adding, losing, or replacing a transmitter under particular conditions. This process is called neurotransmitter switching or respecification. It challenges the assumption that a mature neuron's chemical identity is permanently fixed after development. The evidence does not mean that all neurons routinely change transmitter type or that identity is arbitrary. Rather, it shows that at least some neuronal properties remain responsive to activity and context even after a cell has matured.
Study instruction:
- "Define neurotransmitter switching and explain how it differs from simply changing the amount of transmitter released by a neuron."
Key point:
- Some mature neurons can change aspects of their neurotransmitter identity.
- Neuronal identity is more flexible than once assumed, but switching is not universal.
Source links:
- Spitzer: Neurotransmitter Switching? No Surprise
26. Q: How could a change in neurotransmitter identity alter a neural circuit?
A: Show answer
Different neurotransmitters can have different effects on the cells receiving them. A shift in the transmitter released at a synapse may therefore change whether that connection tends to excite or inhibit its target, depending on the receptor system and circuit context. Such a change could alter local network balance and influence how information flows through a circuit. The chapter notes that some neurons may convert synaptic effects from excitatory to inhibitory or vice versa. This is a significant form of plasticity because it changes the chemical nature of communication, not merely its strength. The exact effect depends on the transmitter, receptors, developmental stage, and target cell.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how changing a transmitter can alter circuit function even if the anatomical connection remains in place."
Key point:
- Neurotransmitter switching can change the functional effect of an existing synaptic connection.
- The outcome depends on receptor expression and the surrounding circuit, not transmitter identity alone.
Source links:
- Spitzer: Neurotransmitter Switching? No Surprise
27. Q: Why does the chapter say that mature neurons may not have an entirely fixed identity?
A: Show answer
Traditional classifications often treat a mature neuron as belonging to a stable chemical or electrical category. The chapter reviews research suggesting that some mature neurons can alter neurotransmitter expression or electrical firing properties in response to activity. This means that cell identity may include features that remain adjustable rather than being permanently locked at the end of development. The claim is limited: many aspects of neuronal identity remain stable, and not every cell type has been shown to switch. The important revision is that maturation does not necessarily end all functional adaptation. Neural cells can retain some capacity to change how they communicate and participate in networks.
Study instruction:
- "List two properties once assumed to be fixed in mature neurons and describe the evidence that challenges that assumption."
Key point:
- Some mature neuronal properties can remain activity-dependent and modifiable.
- Evidence for flexibility in selected properties does not mean that all features of neuronal identity are fluid.
Source links:
- Spitzer: Neurotransmitter Switching? No Surprise
- Dehorter et al.: Activity-Dependent Interneuron Properties
28. Q: What are basket cells, and why are fast-spiking and slow-spiking properties relevant?
A: Show answer
Basket cells are inhibitory interneurons that make connections around the cell bodies of other neurons and help regulate local circuit activity. The chapter discusses fast-spiking and slow-spiking properties as ways of classifying interneuron activity patterns. These electrical characteristics influence how quickly and repeatedly the cells can fire, which affects the timing and strength of inhibition within a network. The chapter notes that activity can alter these properties, suggesting that some interneurons are not permanently locked into one firing category. This matters because inhibitory circuits help regulate the balance of activity and can influence the timing of developmental plasticity and the overall dynamics of neural networks.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how changing the firing properties of an inhibitory interneuron could alter the activity of the local circuit it regulates."
Key point:
- Basket cells are inhibitory interneurons that help regulate local neural activity.
- Their firing properties can influence circuit timing and the balance between excitation and inhibition.
Source links:
- Dehorter et al.: Activity-Dependent Interneuron Properties
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
29. Q: How can neuronal activity change the firing properties of fast-spiking interneurons?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes research showing that neuronal activity can alter the properties of fast-spiking interneurons through an activity-dependent transcriptional mechanism. A protein associated with this process enters the nucleus and influences gene expression, including the expression of potassium channels that help determine firing behavior. The resulting changes can shift the functional properties of interneurons rather than leaving them in a fixed category. This is important because potassium-channel expression affects how a neuron responds to input and generates action potentials. The study supports the idea that mature circuit elements can adjust their electrical properties in response to network activity, although the mechanism and scope are specific to the cells studied.
Study instruction:
- "Trace the sequence from network activity to transcriptional regulation, ion-channel expression, and altered firing properties."
Key point:
- Activity-dependent gene regulation can change interneuron firing characteristics.
- Electrical properties are partly shaped by molecular mechanisms that remain responsive to activity.
Source links:
- Dehorter et al.: Activity-Dependent Interneuron Properties
30. Q: What is the significance of Er81 in the chapter's discussion of interneuron plasticity?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a protein that enters the nucleus and regulates expression of potassium channels, which influence a neuron's firing rate. Research by Dehorter and colleagues identified the transcriptional regulator Er81 as important in the activity-dependent tuning of fast-spiking interneuron properties. Changes in Er81 expression help define a spectrum of interneuron characteristics and can alter the relative distribution of functional properties within a cortical network. The broader significance is that gene regulation can translate patterns of network activity into changes in neuronal physiology. This challenges a rigid view of cell categories while showing how molecular events can influence circuit-level behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a transcriptional regulator can connect changes in network activity with altered ion-channel expression and neuronal firing."
Key point:
- Er81 is involved in activity-dependent tuning of fast-spiking interneuron properties.
- Transcriptional regulation can translate experience or activity into functional changes in neural circuits.
Source links:
- Dehorter et al.: Activity-Dependent Interneuron Properties
31. Q: Why could shifts in the proportion of fast- and slow-spiking interneurons affect a neural network?
A: Show answer
Inhibitory interneurons regulate when and how strongly other neurons can fire. If the relative proportion of interneurons with different firing properties changes, the timing, duration, and distribution of inhibition within a network may also change. This can affect synchrony, information flow, and the balance between excitation and inhibition. The chapter suggests that activity-dependent tuning of interneuron properties could alter population dynamics by changing the mixture of fast- and slow-spiking cells. The implication is not that a single cell-type ratio determines all network function, but that the functional properties of inhibitory cells are one adjustable component of circuit organization.
Study instruction:
- "Describe how changing inhibitory timing could alter the output of a group of connected neurons, using fast- and slow-spiking properties as examples."
Key point:
- Interneuron firing properties help shape the timing and balance of local network activity.
- Activity-dependent changes in cell properties can influence population dynamics beyond the individual neuron.
Source links:
- Dehorter et al.: Activity-Dependent Interneuron Properties
32. Q: Why is there still no single general theory that explains all neuroplasticity?
A: Show answer
Neuroplasticity includes many mechanisms that operate at different levels, timescales, and brain regions. Synaptic strengthening, synapse elimination, myelin remodeling, transmitter switching, and large-scale network changes may be related, but researchers do not yet know how they are coordinated in every situation. A given experience may trigger several changes, or a particular mechanism may operate independently. The chapter emphasizes that studying one level at a time makes it difficult to determine how molecular events combine to produce behavior and thought. The absence of a single theory does not mean that nothing is known; it means that the field is still integrating diverse findings into a comprehensive framework.
Study instruction:
- "Identify at least three distinct mechanisms of plasticity and explain why one theory may not yet account for their different time courses and functions."
Key point:
- Neuroplasticity is a collection of diverse processes rather than one unified mechanism.
- Researchers are still working to connect cellular, network, and behavioral levels into a broader theory.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
33. Q: Why is it difficult to connect molecular changes directly to behavior and thought?
A: Show answer
A molecular change can influence receptor function, synaptic transmission, cell excitability, local circuit activity, and broader network coordination before it contributes to behavior. Each stage is shaped by other signals and environmental conditions. The chapter notes that researchers can examine cellular changes in experimental animals and use neuroimaging to study large-scale human brain organization, but cannot yet measure all levels simultaneously with sufficient detail. As a result, it can be difficult to determine which molecular changes are most closely related to a particular behavior or which scale of connectivity is most informative. Strong explanations therefore distinguish plausible mechanisms from directly demonstrated behavioral effects.
Study instruction:
- "Build a causal chain from a molecular event to a possible behavioral outcome and mark where additional evidence would be required."
Key point:
- Behavior emerges from multiple levels between molecular events and whole-person performance.
- Current methods do not allow every level of plasticity to be measured simultaneously in a living human brain.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
34. Q: What is meant by the brain connectome, and why is it relevant to neuroplasticity?
A: Show answer
The connectome is a description or map of connections within the nervous system, often considered at multiple scales. The chapter describes the brain as a network containing many interconnected hubs and notes the effort to map its organization. Neuroplasticity matters because connections can change in strength, number, timing, and sometimes structural organization. A connectome is therefore not simply a permanent wiring diagram; it is a model of connectivity that must account for stability and change. Mapping connections can help researchers understand how information is distributed, but a static map alone cannot explain all activity, learning, or individual behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Define the connectome and explain why a map of connections must be interpreted alongside information about activity and plastic change."
Key point:
- The connectome describes brain connectivity across multiple scales.
- Connectivity maps need to account for both stable organization and experience-dependent change.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
35. Q: Why does the chapter distinguish connectivity at smaller and larger scales?
A: Show answer
At smaller scales, individual synapses and local connections can change continually as neurons adapt to activity. At larger scales, long-range pathways and major network organization may appear more stable, although they can also change over longer periods. The chapter uses this distinction to explain why a brain may show substantial local plasticity while its broad anatomical organization remains relatively recognizable. Both scales matter: local changes can influence the activity of larger networks, and long-range pathways constrain how local regions communicate. Studying only one scale risks missing important interactions between local flexibility and global organization.
Study instruction:
- "Compare local synaptic connectivity with long-range white-matter connectivity and give one example of how changes at one scale might influence the other."
Key point:
- Local connectivity can be highly dynamic while large-scale organization is relatively more stable.
- Both scales interact and are needed to understand network function.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
36. Q: Why is it difficult to decide which scale of connectivity is most useful for understanding behavior?
A: Show answer
Behavior depends on coordinated activity across multiple levels, from molecular events and synapses to local circuits and long-range networks. A measurement that captures one scale may miss changes occurring at another. For example, a stable long-range tract may carry signals through synapses whose strengths are changing, while a local circuit change may have effects that spread across a larger network. The chapter argues that researchers still need to determine which types of connectivity changes are most closely related to particular behaviors. The most useful scale may depend on the task, brain system, and question being asked rather than one universally optimal map.
Study instruction:
- "For a chosen behavior, identify at least two connectivity scales that could matter and explain what each measurement would reveal or miss."
Key point:
- No single connectivity scale is sufficient for every behavioral question.
- The relevant scale depends on the neural system and the behavior being studied.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
37. Q: What do individual differences tell us about neuroplasticity?
A: Show answer
The chapter argues that brains differ substantially because each person has a unique developmental and experiential history. People may therefore respond differently to the same experience, with different amounts or types of plastic change. Differences in genetics, health, prior learning, age, environment, and network organization can all influence the result. This means that an average group finding should not be treated as a guaranteed outcome for every individual. It also challenges the idea of a single “textbook brain” against which all people should be measured. Understanding plasticity requires attention to both shared principles and individual variation.
Study instruction:
- "List several factors that could cause two people to show different neural changes after the same training experience."
Key point:
- Plasticity varies across individuals as well as across brain regions and developmental stages.
- Group averages do not predict every individual's response to an experience or intervention.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Huber et al.: Experience-Dependent Plasticity After Recovered Sight
38. Q: Why does the chapter say that there is no such thing as a “textbook brain”?
A: Show answer
A textbook brain is an idealized average that cannot fully represent the range of human neural organization. Each person's brain has been shaped by development, sensory experience, learning, health, environment, and personal history. Even when two people share broad anatomical features, their detailed connectivity and functional organization may differ. The chapter suggests that these differences may be greater than commonly assumed and that each brain is, to some extent, custom-built for the demands placed on it. This does not mean that neuroscience cannot identify shared patterns; it means that shared patterns must be interpreted alongside individual variation rather than treated as an exact template for everyone.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how a population-level model can be useful while still failing to describe every individual's neural organization."
Key point:
- Human brains share broad principles but differ in their detailed structure and functional history.
- Averages are useful models, not exact templates for every person.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
39. Q: How can the same experience produce different amounts or types of plasticity in different people?
A: Show answer
The same experience is processed by brains with different histories, developmental stages, health conditions, prior skills, and network organizations. One person may already have a strong pathway relevant to the task, while another may need to recruit different circuits or learn a different strategy. Differences in attention, motivation, sleep, stress, and biological factors can also influence the neural response. The chapter proposes that individual brains may differ in their capacity for plastic change, so the same experience may produce different extents and patterns of adaptation. This variability is not necessarily a failure of learning; it is a normal feature of individualized nervous systems.
Study instruction:
- "Compare two hypothetical learners with different prior experience and predict how their neural adaptation might differ without assuming a fixed outcome."
Key point:
- Prior experience and individual biology influence how the brain responds to new experiences.
- Different plastic outcomes do not necessarily mean that one learner is succeeding and another is failing.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
40. Q: What does the case of M.M. illustrate about restoring vision after prolonged blindness?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an individual who had been blind from early childhood until vision was restored in one eye in adulthood. Despite the restoration of visual input, the person continued to have major difficulties with object, face, and three-dimensional form recognition. Follow-up research found little evidence that the high-level visual organization normally associated with these functions had recovered after more than a decade of visual experience. The case illustrates that restoring sensory input does not automatically restore every function that depends on experience during earlier developmental periods. It highlights the lasting influence of critical-period plasticity while remaining a single case rather than a universal rule for all visual recovery.
Study instruction:
- "Summarize the intervention, the functions that remained impaired, and the limitation of drawing broad conclusions from one case."
Key point:
- Restoring visual input in adulthood did not fully restore high-level visual functions in the reported case.
- The case illustrates developmental constraints but cannot predict every person's recovery.
Source links:
- Huber et al.: Experience-Dependent Plasticity After Recovered Sight
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
41. Q: Why did prolonged adult visual experience not fully reverse the deficits described in the M.M. case?
A: Show answer
The chapter links the outcome to the fact that some visual circuits are shaped during critical periods of early development. When the relevant experience is absent for many years, the normal organization of higher-level visual processing may not develop or may become difficult to establish later. Restoring the eye's ability to receive input does not automatically recreate all the neural computations and category-selective organization that ordinarily develop through early experience. The case therefore distinguishes restoration of sensory access from restoration of complex perception. It does not prove that adult visual plasticity is absent; rather, it shows that some functions may remain constrained after prolonged deprivation.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish restoration of peripheral sensory input from recovery of the higher-level neural functions that interpret that input."
Key point:
- Sensory access and complex perceptual recovery are different outcomes.
- Some developmental constraints may persist even after the original sensory input is restored.
Source links:
- Huber et al.: Experience-Dependent Plasticity After Recovered Sight
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
42. Q: What does the M.M. case not prove about adult neuroplasticity?
A: Show answer
The case does not prove that adult brains cannot change, that all adults with sensory deprivation will have the same outcome, or that rehabilitation is futile. The chapter presents it as an example of limits associated with a specific visual function after prolonged deprivation. Other adult systems and tasks show measurable plasticity, including motor learning and activity-dependent myelination. A single case can reveal an important constraint but cannot establish a universal law. The correct interpretation is that the timing and type of experience matter, and that some functions may be harder to recover after critical developmental windows have passed.
Study instruction:
- "Write one conclusion that is supported by the M.M. case and one broader conclusion that would exceed the evidence."
Key point:
- The M.M. case shows limits in a particular form of adult visual recovery.
- It does not negate adult plasticity in other systems or establish that all recovery is impossible.
Source links:
- Huber et al.: Experience-Dependent Plasticity After Recovered Sight
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
43. Q: How should researchers combine animal experiments, neuroimaging, and behavioral testing when studying plasticity?
A: Show answer
Animal experiments can reveal cellular mechanisms under controlled conditions, such as how activity changes myelin formation or neuronal properties. Human neuroimaging can show structural or functional patterns in living people, while behavioral testing measures whether those patterns relate to actual performance. Each method answers different questions and has limits. Imaging associations do not automatically establish causation, and animal findings do not always translate directly to human experience. Behavioral improvement also does not identify the precise cellular mechanism responsible. Combining methods can provide a stronger account, especially when findings converge across levels, populations, and time points. The chapter emphasizes that simultaneous measurement across all levels remains a major challenge.
Study instruction:
- "Design a simple research plan using one mechanistic method, one human brain measure, and one behavioral outcome, then state what each contributes."
Key point:
- Different methods reveal different levels of plasticity and should be interpreted together.
- Converging evidence is stronger than assuming one measure proves the entire mechanism.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
- Huber et al.: Experience-Dependent Plasticity After Recovered Sight
44. Q: Why should findings from a specific experimental task not be generalized to every kind of learning?
A: Show answer
Plasticity depends on the neural systems recruited by a task, the type of practice, the learner's history, and the timescale of measurement. A motor-skill experiment that reveals activity-dependent myelination does not prove that language learning, emotional learning, or every cognitive task uses the same mechanism to the same extent. Similarly, a study of visual critical periods cannot be applied directly to every sensory or cognitive system. The chapter repeatedly emphasizes that different forms of plasticity occur in specific brain areas and under particular conditions. Generalization should therefore be limited to the populations, tasks, mechanisms, and outcomes actually studied.
Study instruction:
- "Choose one study from the chapter and state what its findings support, what they do not establish, and which new experiment would test broader generalization."
Key point:
- Plasticity mechanisms are task- and system-dependent.
- A finding in one experimental model should not be generalized automatically to all forms of learning.
Source links:
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
45. Q: What does the chapter suggest about the relationship between plasticity and rehabilitation after injury?
A: Show answer
The chapter recognizes that plasticity can support compensation and partial recovery after brain injury, but it warns against promising complete restoration. Recovery depends on the type and location of injury, the functions affected, remaining neural pathways, timing, rehabilitation, and individual differences. Some changes may involve strengthening alternative pathways, reorganizing cortical representations, or altering myelination and network activity. The chapter also notes that attempts to enhance plasticity for recovery remain experimental in some areas and may produce modest benefits. The practical implication is that rehabilitation should be evidence-based and individualized, with realistic goals rather than claims that the brain can always repair any damage.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three factors that influence recovery after injury and explain why a plasticity-based intervention needs evidence of meaningful functional benefit."
Key point:
- Neuroplasticity can support compensation and recovery, but outcomes vary and may be incomplete.
- Rehabilitation should be individualized and judged by meaningful function, not by claims of “rewiring” alone.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Hensch: Critical Period Mechanisms in Developing Visual Cortex
46. Q: How can neuroplasticity have negative consequences as well as positive ones?
A: Show answer
Plasticity is the nervous system's capacity to adapt, not a guarantee that every adaptation is beneficial. Strengthened synapses and learned associations can support useful skills, but they can also reinforce maladaptive habits or persistent symptoms. The chapter points to addiction and certain chronic pain conditions as examples in which synaptic changes contribute to harmful patterns. Excessive or mistimed change during development can also have lasting effects. The same general principle—experience modifies neural circuits—can therefore lead to different outcomes depending on the circuit, timing, intensity, and context. This is why plasticity should be evaluated by its functional consequences rather than celebrated as inherently positive.
Study instruction:
- "Give one beneficial and one harmful example of plasticity and identify the circuit or function affected in each."
Key point:
- Plasticity can support learning and recovery but can also reinforce maladaptive patterns.
- The value of a neural change depends on its context and consequences.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
47. Q: What is the importance of activity-dependent changes in neuronal identity and myelination for the broader theory of brain function?
A: Show answer
The chapter's examples suggest that neural function is shaped not only by synaptic strength but also by the properties of the cells and glia that make up a circuit. Activity can influence oligodendrocyte myelin formation, alter the number of myelin sheaths, and tune the electrical properties or neurotransmitter identity of some neurons. These findings expand the concept of plasticity beyond a simple model in which fixed neurons merely strengthen or weaken their synapses. They suggest that network adaptation may involve coordinated changes in neurons, glial cells, and connectivity. The challenge is to determine when these changes occur together and how they influence behavior.
Study instruction:
- "Compare synaptic plasticity, myelin plasticity, and neuronal identity changes as three distinct ways a circuit can adapt."
Key point:
- Plasticity can involve neurons, glial cells, synapses, and network organization.
- The broader theory must integrate several interacting cellular and circuit mechanisms.
Source links:
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
- Spitzer: Neurotransmitter Switching? No Surprise
- Dehorter et al.: Activity-Dependent Interneuron Properties
48. Q: How should the chapter's evidence about plasticity be used in education, training, or self-improvement?
A: Show answer
The evidence supports the idea that sustained, relevant experience can change neural systems, but it does not justify exaggerated promises about rapid transformation. Training should be specific to the skill or function being developed, and progress should be measured by meaningful performance rather than by claims that the brain has been “rewired.” Age, prior knowledge, motivation, health, practice quality, and individual differences all influence outcomes. The chapter also warns that some interventions advertised as brain-enhancing have not been shown to produce broad benefits. A responsible application uses established learning principles, realistic goals, and evidence about the task rather than assuming that any repetitive activity will improve every cognitive ability.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a brain-training or learning claim by asking what skill is measured, how long the intervention lasts, whether gains transfer, and what evidence supports the claim."
Key point:
- Sustained, task-relevant experience can support plastic change and skill learning.
- Plasticity does not validate every brain-training product or guarantee transfer to unrelated abilities.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- McKenzie et al.: Motor Skill Learning Requires Active Central Myelination
49. Q: What is the difference between a structural change and a functional change in neuroplasticity?
A: Show answer
A functional change alters how neurons or circuits operate, such as a shift in firing properties, synaptic effectiveness, or the timing of network activity. A structural change alters physical features such as synapse number, dendritic structure, axonal branching, or myelin distribution. The two can interact: functional changes may precede structural remodeling, and structural changes can alter later signaling. The chapter gives examples of rapid changes in electrical properties and slower changes in myelin or connectivity. Distinguishing these categories helps researchers specify what was measured and avoids assuming that an observed change in activity necessarily reflects a permanent anatomical alteration.
Study instruction:
- "Classify examples from the chapter as functional, structural, or involving both, and explain why the distinction matters."
Key point:
- Functional plasticity changes how circuits operate; structural plasticity changes their physical organization.
- The two forms can interact but should not be treated as interchangeable.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
- Mensch et al.: Synaptic Vesicle Release and Myelin Sheath Number
- Dehorter et al.: Activity-Dependent Interneuron Properties
50. Q: Why is individual brain history important when interpreting a person's abilities or limitations?
A: Show answer
A person's current brain organization reflects development, sensory experience, learning, health, injury, and environment across time. Two people with similar test scores or diagnoses may have reached those outcomes through different neural pathways. Conversely, two people with similar experiences may show different levels of adaptation because their prior histories and biological conditions differ. The chapter argues that each brain is shaped by the demands placed on it and is therefore partly unique. This perspective encourages careful assessment rather than assuming that one average pattern explains every person. It also reminds researchers and educators that individual variation is a central feature of human neuroplasticity.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how prior learning and developmental history could change the interpretation of the same performance result in two individuals."
Key point:
- Individual brain organization reflects a unique history of experience and development.
- Shared test outcomes do not necessarily imply identical neural organization or learning needs.
Source links:
- MIT Press: Neuroplasticity by Moheb Costandi
51. Q: What are the most important lessons to remember from the book's conclusion on neuroplasticity?
A: Show answer
The conclusion presents the brain as a dynamic system that changes across the lifespan through many interacting mechanisms. Plasticity includes synaptic strengthening and weakening, the creation and elimination of connections, changes in myelin, shifts in neuronal properties, and reorganization of networks. Development often offers heightened opportunities for some forms of change, but adult plasticity remains possible. The chapter also stresses limits: plasticity is not unlimited, a single intervention cannot guarantee recovery, and changes may be harmful as well as helpful. Individual brains differ because of their unique histories. The central lesson is to understand neuroplasticity as diverse, context-dependent, and constrained rather than as a simple promise of unlimited change.
Study instruction:
- "Review the chapter's mechanisms, developmental timing, myelin findings, neuronal identity examples, connectome discussion, and M.M. case; then explain how they support a balanced view of lifelong plasticity."
Key point:
- Neuroplasticity is lifelong, diverse, and shaped by experience, development, and individual history.
- It enables adaptation but remains limited, context-dependent, and capable of both beneficial and harmful outcomes.
